And he who is pointing out the fault should understand that it is also his duty to advance in Krsna consciousness. That requires that he also see the good. If he cannot see the good in others, a Vaisnava should not point out their faults. Otherwise, he will not be able to correct others with the beneficence that Lord Siva felt when he corrected Daksa.

If we want to point out the faults in others, we should also first realize that the same faults exist in ourselves. There is a saying in English: “People who live in glass houses shouldn’t throw rocks! ” It is better to work on one’s own faults than to concentrate on the faults of others. We should not become professional faultfinders. If we find ourselves criticizing others, we should ask ourselves why we are unable to see the good in them.

If we wish to improve our critical mentalities, we should associate with those who have learned to see the good in others. We should not only associate with them, but we should serve them, praying to develop their qualities.

A devotee is always the servant of other devotees. If we say we can’t find anyone more advanced than ourselves to serve, we had better look at our problems and understand our true position. Do we have the characteristics of an advanced devotee? Best to seek out faults at the source: our own hearts. Then we will be able to see how others are so much better than ourselves and will push aside the tendency to find fault with them. In this way, we will all go forward in Krsna consciousness.

The Krsna consciousness movement is meant to facilitate forward spiritual progress. It is not meant to be an institution where we push everyone down. When we are envious, we push others down because we think it is the only way we can push ourselves forward. We must learn to control our mind and senses and thus curb envy, pride and lust.

## How can we obtain freedom from attachment to material designations?

**SB** [**4.11.11**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-4/11.11) **Kaunas, September 1995**

Srila Prabhupada explains in his purport to *Bhagavad-gita* ([9.11](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/9.11)):

“Jiva Gosvami, commenting on this verse, says that Krsna, in His plenary expansion as Paramatma, is situated in the moving and the nonmoving entities as the Supersoul, so any neophyte devotee who simply gives his attention to the *arca-murti,* the form of the Supreme Lord in the temple, and does not respect other living entities is uselessly worshiping the form of the Lord in the temple. There are three kinds of devotees of the Lord, and the neophyte is in the lowest stage. The neophyte devotee gives more attention to the Deity in the temple than to other devotees, so Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura warns that this sort of mentality should be corrected. A devotee should see that because Krsna is present in everyone’s heart as Paramatma, every body is the embodiment or the temple of the Supreme Lord; so as one offers respect to the temple of the Lord, he should similarly properly respect each and every body in which the Paramatma dwells. Everyone should therefore be given proper respect and should not be neglected. ”

In the *Bhagavatam’s* fifth canto, Lord Rsabhadeva instructs His sons as follows:

**“My dear sons, you should not envy any living entity—be he moving or nonmoving. Knowing that I am situated in them, you should offer respect to all of them at every moment. In this way, you offer respect to Me. ” (SB** [**5.5.26**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-5/5.26)**)**

In other words, we must learn to see Krsna in the heart of every living being. In the eleventh canto Srila Jiva Gosvami is quoted as saying that if a devotee cannot respect other devotees, it will be impossible for him to respect all living beings:

“According to Srila Jiva Gosvami, since a *kanistha-adhikari* who cannot properly respect other devotees will certainly fail to offer respects to ordinary living entities who are not even devotees, a *kanistha-adhikari* is useless for practical preaching work unless he comes to a higher platform of understanding. Jiva Gosvami says, *iyam ca sraddha na sastrarthavadharana-jata.* Because the *kanistha-adhikari s* faith is not actually based on the statements of Vedic literature, he cannot understand the exalted position of the Supreme Personality of Godhead within everyone’s heart. Therefore he cannot actually manifest love of Godhead, nor can he understand the exalted position of the devotees of the Lord. Krsna is so glorious that Krsna’s intimate associates must also be glorious. But this is unknown to a *kanistha-adhikari.* Similarly, the essential qualification of a Vaisnava, which is to offer all respects to others *(amanina manadena kirtaniyah sada harih)* \[Cc. Adi [17.31](https://vedarama.com/en/books/cc-adi/17.31)\] is also conspicuous by its absence in a *kanistha-adhikari.* If such a person, however, has faith in the Vedic literatures and tries to understand the statements of *Bhagavad-gita* and *Srimad-Bhagavatam,* he will gradually be elevated to the secondand first-class stages of devotional service. ” (SB [11.2.47](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-11/2.47), purport)

Srila Jiva Gosvami’s commentary expresses the absolute necessity for a devotee on the third platform of devotional service to rise to the second-class platform if he is to become an effective preacher. This means that a devotee must be able to perceive the presence of the Lord within the heart of all living beings and, because He is within the heart, offer respects to everyone. A devotee is never disrespectful to anyone, because disrespecting others would be disrespecting Krsna within their hearts.

Daksa considered Lord Siva’s tolerance and natural respect as a weakness, but actually it is an exalted quality. What he perceived as negative was actually a sign of Lord Siva’s greatness. Because Lord Siva sees the Supreme Lord in the heart of all living entities, he is both merciful and kind toward them and always prepared to offer them respect. Lord Siva was equally prepared to offer Daksa respect, even after Daksa offended him, but because Daksa had to be corrected of his offensive mentality, Lord Siva also punished him.

What stopped Daksa from offering Lord Siva respect and then to insult him was his pride. Pride is the symptom of a *kanistha-adhikari,* a third-class devotee. Srila Jiva Gosvami explains that a *kanistha-adhikari* cannot see Krsna in the heart. He sees Krsna only in the temple. Without being able to respect devotees, a *kanistha-adhikari* will not be able to respect other living entities, and therefore he will not be able to preach. He will not be able to give up this tendency because he considers himself an advanced devotee.

The purport to *Srimad-Bhagavatam* ([11.2.47](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-11/2.47)) continues: “Another symptom of a *kanistha-adhikari* is that he is infatuated by the material qualification of so-called great materialistic persons. Having a bodily concept of life himself, he is attracted by material opulence and thus minimizes the supreme position of Lord Visnu. ”

This means that a *kanistha-adhikari* measures the qualities of others by material standards because he is still in the bodily concept of life. Since devotional qualities cannot be measured by material standards, a *kanistha-adhikari* is unable to appreciate advanced devotees.

A *kanistha-adhikari* cannot imagine anyone more pious or religious than himself. Thus he cannot recognize the advancement of the *madhyamaor uttama-bhaktas.* Sometimes out of false pride he criticizes, neglects, or simply misunderstands more advanced devotees or their positions as preachers and self-realized souls. Some *kanistha-adhikaris* have material qualifications—they may know how to do something useful in devotional service that is well by material standards—but the *kanistha-adhikari* mistakes this expertise for spiritual qualification. He thinks, “Everyone should see how qualified I am. ” Perhaps he is expert in construction. When he sees others with less knowledge of construction, he considers them inferior. Neither does he like it when those more advanced devotees do not seem to appreciate his position. “You are not recognizing my abilities! ” He becomes even more critical and condescending. He is unable to recognize the true basis of spiritual advancement.

When we think that advancement in spiritual life means being recognized by being offered a position, we are in trouble. Position is a symbol of status, and if we are neophytes, we will take it not as an opportunity to increase our service to the Vaisnavas, but as a sign that we are superior to them. Prestigious positions exist all over the world. Are they signs of spiritual advancement? Someone is in charge of a factory. He spends his days ordering perhaps thousands of people around. Does that mean he is spiritually advanced? There are symptoms of spiritual advancement, but attaining a position is not one of them. Rather, positions are based on material designations, and in that sense, they are worthless.

One of the symptoms of *bhakti* is *sarvopadhi-vinirmuktam tatparatvena nirmalam:* all material designations are destroyed. Those who are advanced in devotional service are free from attachment to material designation. They do not consider themselves superior to others because they have a position over them. Rather, they consider others their superiors. In this way, an advanced devotee actually becomes superior to others. He shows his superiority by his humble dealings, not by the external label he wears.

An advanced devotee treats others with respect. He not only respects devotees but *all* living entities. If someone professing to be a devotee cannot even respect other devotees, if he’s always looking down on devotees and criticizing them, then he is not in a very high position.

A *kanistha* finds faults in others, then labels and characterizes them according to their faults. He has a label for everyone within his mind. Because of those labels, he finds himself unable to be respectful. “This one is not sincere. That one is lazy. This one is too attached, and that one is always thinking of sex. ” He never sees the surrender these devotees exhibit, and he doesn’t understand that a Vaisnava’s nature is to think of how to elevate others, not tear them down. A more advanced devotee manifests his superiority by helping others, not by hurting them. A *kanistha-adhikari* thinks that by pointing out the faults in others, his job is done. He is not concerned with helping them overcome their faults. It is only by becoming Krsna conscious that faults are destroyed. *Yasyasti bhaktir bhagavaty akincana, sarvair gunais tatra samasate surah:* All the good qualities of the demigods appear in a devotee. When we take to devotional service, we automatically develop good qualities and our bad qualities gradually vanish.

This only happens by the mercy of a devotee—someone who can see the good in us, see Krsna in our heart, and offer us the respect of Krsna conscious help. Krsna takes away all the material attachments of a person who develops an urge to hear about Him *(srnvatam svakathah krsnah punya-sravana-kirtanah).* Krsna personally cleanses the impurities from the heart of all those who hear of His qualities and pastimes. Are we helping the process by simply criticizing people? We can help by guiding them to hear about Krsna so Krsnacan cleanse their heart.

Therefore we say that we become more advanced by associating with those who are more advanced. We can recognize those who are more advanced by the following characteristics: they understand that Krsna is situated in every living entity’s heart. Therefore, they understand that each living entity has some good. They decide to see it. Then they fan the spark of that potential Krsna consciousness and offer encouragement. Because Krsna is situated in the heart, they offer respect.

Sometimes neophyte devotees think that nondevotees have superior qualities when they see so many disrespectful dealings among the devotees and act disrespectfully themselves. They begin to appreciate the material qualities of nondevotees because they cannot appreciate the spiritual qualities of devotees. We must overcome this *kanistha* platform.

If we wish to overcome it, we must be careful not to unnecessarily criticize others. We should be honest with ourselves. We should understand that if we are mentally absorbed in the other devotees’ faults, we will not be able to appreciate their good qualities, and without being able to see the good in them or in others, we will not be able to preach. We should learn to take shelter of those who are more advanced so that we can elevate our own consciousness. That is our only chance.

## Proper behavior is just as important as preaching

**SB** [**4.11.11**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-4/11.11) **Kaunas, September 1995**

Srila Rupa Gosvami states in the *Upadesamrta* that an inexperienced devotee should ask questions of an experienced devotee. We are meant to reveal our minds in confidence. We inquire in confidence because we have faith that the person we are inquiring from is trustworthy. What makes us feel that trust? We are certain that the superior devotee is trying to help us. Even if the superior reprimands us, we can accept it as Krsna’s mercy.

Exchanges between devotees must be based on respect. When a superior devotee reprimands us or points out our faults, we hear his words because we respect him. Srila Prabhupada said we should not demand respect but command it. A Vaisnava never demands respect. He never says, “Don’t you see my position? Respect me! ” Rather, he commands respect by his pure behavior. We feel compelled to respect a devotee who shows proper Krsna conscious dealings with others. When we see someone with proper behavior, we think of him as a preacher. His behavior is a symptom of his advancement.

Sanatana Gosvami told Haridasa Thakura, “Some people preach, but they don’t behave properly. Some people behave nicely, but they don’t preach. But you, Haridasa Thakura, both preach and behave perfectly. Therefore, you are the spiritual master of the entire world. ” Haridasa Thakura set a perfect example, and even from his exalted state, he respected all living entities.

An example of Haridasa Thakura’s respectful nature was his relationship with the Kazi. The Kazi had him beaten in twenty-two marketplaces and then thrown in the river. When he emerged from the river, he continued chanting the Lord’s holy name. The

Kazi heard that Haridasa Thakura had survived and went to see for himself. When he saw Haridasa Thakura chanting on the streets, he understood that he must truly be a saintly person. He prostrated himself before Haridasa Thakura and begged forgiveness. Haridasa Thakura said, “Actually I should have been beaten more than I was. That punishment was not sufficient, I have listened to blasphemy of the Supreme Lord and I did not immediately give up my life. ”

Haridasa Thakura did not find fault with the Kazi’s behavior but examined his own fault and saw it as the cause of his suffering. He told the Kazi, “If I am beaten, it is due to my offenses. I know if anyone is beaten in this way, it is because of his own offenses. You are not beating me; Krsna is beating me. ”

This pastime reveals Haridasa Thakura’s exalted position and shows how he was able to respect Krsna’s presence within the hearts of all. The symptom of an advanced devotee is that he is able to see Krsna in everyone’s heart and in everything, and because he sees that, he is able to preach. That is also how we lift ourselves off the *kanistha-adhikari* platform: we preach Krsna consciousness to others and establish ourselves in a superior devotional position by preaching and proper behavior.

Proper behavior is just as important as preaching. Srila Prabhupada said that example is better than precept. How can we set a good example? We should never lose our good sense. We should never, for example, call devotees ill names or insult them in other ways. There is no place for such behavior in *Vaisnava sanga.*

But what happens when we think ill of others because of our own pride? We lose our ability to discriminate. We lose our good sense and then let our polluted thoughts come out of our mouths. We often know we shouldn’t speak something, but we say it anyway. Neither do we feel ecstasy after offending Vaisnavas. “Oh, what ecstasy! I’ve just blasphemed a devotee! That made me feel really good! I feel like I'm advancing! ” No, if we have any good sense left in us, we’ll immediately repent our sharp tongue.

That repentance is our only hope. If we don’t repent, we become degraded. A devotee should never be offensive in his behavior toward anyone. Therefore, what is the necessity of calling others ill names? We should not think this is our *“lila”.* We will never read of such *“lilas”* in the *Caitanya-caritamrta.* Ill names come out of our mouth not because we are experiencing spontaneous devotion but because we are proud. Those who are proud cannot appreciate the good qualities of others. They have no good sense.

If we feel pride arising in our mind, we should be patient. What is the basis of this patience? We should know that Krsna will take care of us. This is a fundamental understanding.

## We must be patient in practicing devotional service

**Bhaktyaloka Moscow**

I would like to read from Srila Bhaktivinoda Thakura’s *Bhaktyaloka* on the quality of *dhairya,* patience. Patience is extremely important when practicing devotional service. Those who possess *dhairya* are called *dhira,* sober. When we lack this quality, we become restless. The impatient cannot work. Patience allows a practitioner to control first himself, then the whole world.

The first verse of *Sri Upadesamrta,* on which *Bhaktyaloka* is a commentary, describes the symptoms of *dhairya* as follows:

*vaco vegam manasah krodha-vegam*  
*jihva-vegam udaropastha-vegam*  
*etan vegan yo visaheta dhirah*  
*sarvam apimam prthivim sa sisyat*

**“A sober person who can tolerate the urge to speak, the mind’s demands, the actions of anger and the urges of the tongue, belly and genitals is qualified to make disciples all over the world. ”**

Srila Bhaktivinoda Thakura states that for a person practicing devotional service, patience is extremely necessary. I have seen this personally in my preaching to devotees. Many are restless because they feel they have not made enough spiritual advancement. Why, they wonder, are they not yet pure devotees? Why are they still experiencing trouble with the mind? Why haven’t they been knocked off their feet by ecstasy? After all, they have been practicing Krsna consciousness for three whole years. How long will they have to wait for “it” to happen?

Bhaktivinoda Thakura says that if we want to practice devotional service, we must have patience. One who can be patient is sober. Although he quotes the first verse of *Upadesamrta* in his commentary as an explanation of *dhairya,* the word *dhairya* is not mentioned at all in the verse. The word *dhira* is used, however. *Dhira* is sobriety. Patience and sobriety are similar in meaning.

The first verse of *Upadesamrta* speaks about controlling the body’s six demands. A person is *dhira* if he can control the urge to speak, the mind’s demands, and anger. To be *dhira,* he must also be able to control the belly and genitals. Bhaktivinoda Thakura says that the basis of sobriety is patience. Why do we control the urge to speak or our anger? Why do we control the mind? The answer is a fundamental understanding in Krsna consciousness: we control ourselves and become patient because we trust that Krsna will take care of us. If we trust Krsna’s care, we can become peaceful and controlled. Those who are not *dhira* cannot understand that. They seek immediate results. They are impatient, the mind running here and there, making plans. We see that especially when people have certain responsibilities that do not carry immediate results. Their minds run here and there, wondering whether a different endeavor would be more productive. They cannot understand that if they simply execute their assigned duties, duties which have ultimately been assigned by Krsna, then the result will come—on Krsna’s time.

Controlling the mind and the urge to speak take similar types of patience. Sometimes we feel we have to express ourselves because if we don’t, Krsna won’t make the arrangement we have chosen for ourselves.

Bhaktivinoda Thakura explains that with patience a practitioner controls first himself, then the whole world. This is a profound statement. Who can imagine controlling the world with patience? We know that Krsna is the controller of everything; by our patiently engaging everything in His service, we come to control the world. When a person is self-controlled, he becomes powerful and able to influence others. He is not controlled by the whims of others because he is not even controlled by his *own* whims. Rather, he is fixed on his life’s purpose and sees everything in relation to the Supreme. If a person is whimsical, any whimsy that passes him by will tempt him. A whimsical person is by definition open to being tempted by sense gratification. The material world is filled with temptation. The slightest allurement can capture a whimsical person’s attention, whether that allurement arrives in the form of sound, sight, taste, or touch.

Sometimes we see devotees who are not fixed in their devotional service barraged by others, “Prabhu, would you like to do this? Would you like to do that? Prabhu, I know the perfect thing for you to be doing. You should be helping me with such-and-such. ” The devotee becomes so bewildered by all the offers that he ends up doing nothing. He doesn’t know what Krsna wants him to do, so he can only decide based on what he considers in his own best interest. As soon as he starts calculating for his own interest, everything is open.

On the contrary, when a devotee understands that service to Krsna is not based on our self-interest but on Krsna’s arrangement, he is no longer tempted by the many offers of this world and strives only to gratify Krsna’s senses. We may say that the first devotee was being tempted by offers related to service, and that such offers cannot be compared to sense gratification, but because the devotee is approaching them in the mood of seeking his own best interest, they *are* sense gratification for him.

If we try to enjoy Krsna, we will be unsuccessful unless we first pursue Krsna’s pleasure. The only way to “enjoy” Krsna is in devotion. Krsna allows Himself to be enjoyed only by His devotees. The devotees are those who have controlled themselves from enjoying this world and have come to want *only* enjoyment in Krsna’s service. Those who are not self-controlled are controlled by the sense objects around them.

Bhaktivinoda Thakura explains that a devotee controls himself by fixing his consciousness on what Krsna wants him to do. When he is fixed in his determination to follow this order, the whole world comes under his control. This is because he sees everything in relation to his service and everything in relation to Krsna. The *Upadesamrta* says that such a person is “qualified to make disciples all over the world. ” Does this statement mean that he becomes a person qualified to control others and that he doesn’t allow anyone to control him? No. It means that he brings them under the same internal potency under which he is situated. He recognizes that he is controlled by Krsna and Krsna’s pure devotees. Under that control he is no longer disturbed by the constant changes of the material energy. If we are ourselves controlled by matter, we will not be able to “control” others, to bring them under the Lord’s protection.

Ultimately, we are all supposed to be under Krsna’s control. When we use the word “self-control, ” we do not mean that we become controllers exactly, but that we agree to be under Krsna’s control. The *Bhagavad-gita* describes the characteristics of the selfcontrolled: he withdraws his senses like a tortoise withdraws his limbs into its shell. Even though many rivers enter the ocean, the ocean remains unchanged. Similarly, a self-controlled person may experience many desires, but he remains unchanged by them. His basis of self-control is his trust that Krsna is supplying his necessities. Therefore, he can become fixed in his determination to surrender to Krsna.

Recently, a devotee asked me, “If one has an unfulfilled desire, does his desire *have* to be fulfilled? ” Yes, everyone’s desire must be fulfilled. But Krsna can fulfill our desires better than anyone else. It’s not possible to allow our desires to go unfulfilled, but who would we like to have fulfill them? Best we allow Krsna to do that. He can fulfill our desires beyond our expectations. He knows how to fulfill our desires in such a way that we end up happy. Krsna is perfect. But if we try to fulfill our desires separate from Krsna, we may get what we want, but it won’t actually satisfy us. We will still feel unfulfilled. Materialists are constantly having their desires fulfilled. Why, then, aren’t they satisfied?

Krsna can fulfill all desires. We may have been used to having our independent desires satisfied by apparently independent agents, but we did not become wholly satisfied. We remained incomplete.

A devotee trusts Krsna and remains patient. Srila Prabhupada gives the example of a woman who has just been married. She must be patient before she can have a child. By the natural course of events, she will become pregnant and eventually give birth. Similarly, a devotee has to patiently await Krsna’s plan. In the meantime, he executes his duties and controls the six urges that push or force him to act in ways that are not to his benefit.

Based on his trust in Krsna, such a self-controlled practitioner of devotional service can control the whole world; that is, he can make disciples all over the world. Such a devotee is able to teach the secret of self-control to others. Without Krsna consciousness, there is no such thing as complete self-control. There may be many persons who appear austere and renounced, but only a devotee who has the full shelter of Krsna’s lotus feet is completely protected from the six urges.

The example of rivers entering an ocean is appropriate. Many rivers may enter the ocean, but the ocean remains still because the ocean is already full. Even as the rivers appear to increase its depths, the ocean never overflows the shore. Similarly, when a person is full in Krsna consciousness, he is satisfied. Desires may enter, but he does not overflow. Rather, he remains still.

Persons who don’t have the shelter of Krsna’s lotus feet fall down despite their severe austerities. This is because they are not complete. Therefore, even ascetics have to come to the platform of *bhakti* if they want to become successful *tapasvis.* Unless they practice some devotion to Krsna, even if they perform austerities for thousands of years, they will fall down.

Srila Prabhupada told us to judge by the result. Someone may ask how we can say that someone who has been performing austerities for many, many years is not renounced or self-controlled. But what is the result of such self-control? The only way it can be successful is if the person takes up at least some degree of devotional service to Krsna. In *Bhagavad-gita* 17. 28, Krsna describes that those who accept severe austerities and penances but who don’t have devotion cannot achieve perfection: “Anything done as sacrifice, charity or penance without faith in the Supreme, O son of Prtha, is impermanent. It is called *asat* and is useless both in this life and the next. ” People may perform their sacrifices, but if they are not basing their activities in devotion, Krsna states that their austerities are useless.

Krsna calls such austerities “impermanent. ” This means that they will not be with these persons in the next life. Therefore, the austerity was uselessly performed. Krsna encourages us to choose *sat* over *asat.* If we genuinely want to become complete, we must sacrifice only to please Krsna. Then we will receive a permanent result.

Anything we do for Krsna’s pleasure delivers a permanent result and brings us closer to Him in devotion. This is our only protection. There is no other protection in the material world. If we factually want protection from this world’s dangers, Krsna Himself advises that we take shelter in Him. If we take shelter in the demigods, we will not be completely protected, because those who take shelter of the demigods go to the planets of the demigods—an impermanent situation. Those who take shelter of the ancestors go to the planets of the ancestors—another impermanent situation. But those who take shelter in Krsna go to Krsna, and that is the only place of permanence.

It takes patience to gain Krsna’s shelter. If we are patient, Krsna will see what we are willing to tolerate to achieve His feet. In this way we will prove our faith. We must be fully dependent upon Him. We cannot expect immediate results according to our timetables. Patience is essential.

In *Bhaktyaloka,* Bhaktivinoda Thakura will describe each of the six urges and show how patience is essential to conquering all of them. We will not become pure devotees overnight. Then how do we show our patience? We show it by being willing to go on hearing regardless of our life’s circumstances. The *Bhagavatam* gives an example. When Hiranyakasipu was causing havoc in the universe, the demigods approached Lord Visnu for relief. The Lord told them to be patient and that He would eventually descend. In the meantime, “Become My devotees by hearing and chanting about Me and offering Me prayers, for these are certainly meant to award benedictions to all living entities. I know all about the activities of Hiranyakasipu and shall surely stop them very soon. Please wait patiently until that time. ” (SB [7.4.25—26](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-7/4.25)) Krsna always cares for and protects His devotees. We simply have to be patient and increase our surrender to Him. If we can’t depend on Krsna for protection, who can we turn to? Krsna knows that if He doesn’t give us protection, we have no one. He is the Supreme Personality of Godhead. When we depend on Him for protection, He sees our fragility and wants to protect us. Krsna knows that the demigods cannot protect us. We have no one else but Him. Therefore, He feels bound to protect those who approach Him for shelter.

If we are actually dependent upon Him, then we are free to hear and chant His glories without the details of our lives interrupting us. We can trust that Krsna can fulfill every desire we may have and keep us from danger. We can trust that He will do this in such a way that we do not lose our footing in Krsna consciousness. Without Krsna consciousness, the fulfilment of desires brings only suffering. Krsna asks, “Why should I let them drink poison? Although the devotees engage in My service, they want their material desires fulfilled. Why should I allow them to drink such poison? If they drink poison, they will only suffer. Instead I should induce them to take shelter of My lotus feet and then they will forget their foolish desires. ” Even a moment of real turning to Krsna, of offering Him our service free from material motivation, can draw Krsna’s attention to us. Then we can make progress on the Krsna conscious path. Therefore, we should both be patient and confident that the ultimate goal can be achieved.

## Is a devotee’s ultimate goal to go back to Godhead or to take others back to Godhead?

A devotee’s ultimate goal is to do whatever Krsna wants him to do. If Krsna wants him to go back to Godhead, he wants to go. If Krsna wants him to stay, he wants to stay. Pleasing Krsna is a devotee’s ultimate goal. A devotee has no separate interest from Krsna’s interest. Because every living being has a relationship with Krsna, Krsna has a plan for each one of us. Whatever He wants for us, we accept.

Krsna states in *Bhagavad-gita,* “As all surrender unto Me, I reward them accordingly. Everyone follows My path in all respects, O son of Prtha. ” Krsna’s way of revealing His plans to His devotees is dynamic. It is not that Krsna has a plan for each of us, and once having executed his desires through us, He pushes us out of the way so He can use the next person. Krsna has unlimited services for the living entity to perform in this apparently unlimited material world. With each new service, we have a new opportunity to please the Lord.

The Lord and His associates also have dynamic exchanges in the spiritual world. The spiritual world is even more full of variety than the material world. Sometimes people misunderstand that. Those who have no understanding of spiritual life think that there is no change in the spiritual world—that it is eternally blank. Krsna tells us that the spiritual world is both changeless and supreme, but that means that everything there is eternally manifest, not that it is blank. It also means that everything is centered around Krsna. It is only our lack of experience in Krsna consciousness that we don’t feel the dynamism of our relationship with Krsna. A truly Krsna conscious person experiences a continual exchange between himself and the Lord. He knows that Krsna is leading him through a variety of engagements, or variety in his engagement, to help him become more fixed in His service. If we want to experience that variety, we have to be prepared to do what Krsna wants us to do and to see how Krsna will reveal to us our particular path of surrender.

That is called “becoming the Lord’s instrument. ” If we maintain the desire to please Krsna as our constant aspiration, Krsna will reveal to us how it can be done. Devotees often say they don’t know what Krsna wants of them. We may sometimes be confused about our duty. But if we genuinely want to please Krsna and pray constantly in our chanting, “O Krsna, please engage me in Your service, ” Krsna will gradually reveal our service to us. Then when we take up the appointed service, we will gain more and more revelation.

There is distinction between accepting the service that has been revealed to us through our sincere chanting and our desire to have that service, and calculating the difference between that service and the service which would be most pleasing to us. Once we know what Krsna wants of us and are convinced of it, then we should execute that duty patiently and with firm faith that Krsna will give us everything we require to serve Him. We will leave the decision about whether we go back to Godhead or stay in the material world in Krsna’s hands. Our destination won’t matter to us, because for a pure devotee, going back to the spiritual world and staying in the material world are the same; he will associate with Krsna regardless. In this regard, Srila Prabhupada always quoted the verse, *narayana-parah sarve na kutascana bibhyati:* “Devotees solely engaged in the devotional service of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, Narayana, never fear any condition of life. For them the heavenly planets, liberation and the hellish planets are all the same, for such devotees are interested only in the service of the Lord. ” (SB [6.17.28](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-6/17.28))

This verse was spoken by Lord Siva to Parvati after she cursed Citraketu Maharaja. Parvati was shocked that Citraketu Maharaja so willingly accepted her curse to become an *asura.* Parvati wondered what kind of person he could be that he would remain undisturbed by such a terrible curse. Lord Siva assured her that that was the nature of a devotee. A devotee is not afraid of any condition of life because whether he is in heaven, hell, or liberated, he simply serves Krsna.

*I have been serving Krsna for seven years, doing everything I could do. As a result, I have lost my health. I can no longer chant my rounds or read sastra. What should I do? I was doing everything you told me to do. Of course, I was working imperfectly and I have many faults. I want to chant japa, but right now I am unable. I can’t do anything for Krsna.*

I was just with a devotee in Vladivostok who asked me the same question. He has a disease that doesn’t completely paralyze him, but almost. He is simply waiting to die. He wanted to know how he could serve Krsna in his condition. He said, “Many times devotees tell me how fortunate I am because I am forced to completely depend on Krsna. They say all the reactions to my past sinful life are being burned up. But it is very difficult to fix my mind! I cannot read, and I can barely chant. I cannot render any physical service whatsoever. ”

We know that there are no impediments to devotional service. Even in the most difficult situation, a devotee has the ability to desire. Desiring to serve Krsna is all that is required if we wish to advance in Krsna consciousness. I am talking about the sincere desire to remember and serve the Lord. *Smartavyah satatam visnur, vismartavyo na jatucit:* “Krsna is the origin of Lord Visnu. He should always be remembered and never forgotten at any time. All the rules and prohibitions mentioned in the *sastras* should be the servants of these two principles. ”

Everything we do with the body—whether our daily duties, chanting our rounds, vibrating the holy name when we are not chanting our rounds, or anything else—is meant to help us remember Krsna and never forget Him. That’s the goal of everything we do. Therefore, if a devotee sincerely desires to serve Krsna, he has to always keep in mind the exact goal. If the goal is to always remember Krsna and never forget Him, then the devotee can achieve that goal by using whatever Krsna has given him. How can he use what Krsna has not given him? We can only use what Krsna has given us.

The body is compared to a boat that can carry us across the ocean. While we are in this boat, we should use it for Krsna’s service. But service to Krsna does not start in the body and it doesn’t end when the body ends. Service takes place in our consciousness. We have to prepare our consciousness for service to Krsna both within and without the body. That’s what we are meant to accomplish in this life. Some of us may have to prepare to serve Krsna without this material body sooner than others, but we will all eventually have to face this reality. We should face the truth now and prepare ourselves to serve Krsna no matter what condition of life we are in.

We serve first by desire. Therefore Srila Prabhupada explained that no material force can impede devotion. If we actually have the desire to serve Krsna, then whatever is within our ability we can offer. If we are unable due to physical circumstances to render outward service, we can inquire, speak about Krsna, and hear. Hearing and chanting never stops. Our ability to continue hearing and chanting even under difficult circumstances will depend on our desire. How much do we want to use whatever strength we have to speak about Krsna? If we have the desire, Krsna will give us the ability to do it. Then we will remember Krsna and our devotion will increase.

I explained this to the young devotee from Vladivostok. He was lying there with his mother by his side. She too is a devotee, and she asked, “Please give him relevant instructions. ” I went on to explain how to render service within the mind and how a devotee who actually has a situation far worse can still serve Krsna in his mind. The *sastra* is full of examples. Nrsimhananda Brahmacari mentally paved a road for Lord Caitanya in his mind, and there is the story of the *brahmana* and the sweet rice. Meditate on serving Krsna in your mind. The Lord will accept your service. There are no obstacles for those who sincerely desire to serve Him.

## Cultivate a constant and favorable desire to serve the Lord

**Bhaktyaloka Moscow, January 12, 1997**

There are six urges: the urge to speak, the demands of the mind, anger, and the pushings of belly, tongue, and genitals. When someone wants to speak more than required, he tends to speak about others, which creates enmity. Speaking uselessly is the business of a fool; by speaking unnecessarily, materialists waste time and experience distress. Pious people observe *mauna-vrata,* a vow of silence, in order to free themselves of this urge. Those who practice devotional service should not speak unnecessarily. If we feel the urge to speak unnecessarily, better we control it. Aside from topics about Krsna, all other topics are unnecessary.

But to speak about subjects favorable to devotional service is not unnecessary speech. Devotees should speak only about Krsna or Krsna’s service. All other topics are counted in the category of useless speech. Those who can control the urge to speak unnecessarily are sober, *dhira.*

Bhaktivinoda Thakura explains that if we do not regulate our tendency to speak, then we will create enmity among one another through gossip. Usually when we speak about others, we do so only to point out their faults. Sometimes people who do not control their speaking urge regret what they have said. Not only have their words disturbed others, but they have disturbed their own mind. This happens especially when gossip is repeated and the victim of the gossip manages to trace the gossip back to its source. The original speaker may question himself, “Why did I speak like that? Maybe I shouldn’t have said what I said. ” As soon as he begins to feel guilty, he will begin to speak something else—something to compensate for his previous statements. Perhaps he makes contradictory statements. When the contradictions are pointed out, he begins to realize that he has to be careful of what he says. He never knows how his words will be taken or how they are going to either affect others or be misrepresented.

Although this thought process may go on in the minds of many individuals, gossip continues. Why? Because we are pushed by the urge to speak unnecessarily. We all suffer from this pushing, this force that compels us to act. Although logic and experience may help us overcome it, it is difficult to overcome completely. These urges are very powerful.

Therefore, we sometimes see that even those who wish to restrict themselves for some moral reason are unable. It’s a moral principle not to speak ill of others, but still the urge to speak attacks again and again and cannot be controlled. It is natural to want to say something in the presence of others, to engage in light conversation, to ensure others that you are friendly. What better topic than to discuss someone who isn’t present with you? That doesn’t threaten anyone. Rather, it becomes the basis of camaraderie.

Devotees should not engage in this kind of talk. Bhaktivinoda Thakura advises that if we do feel the urge to speak, but we have nothing of value to say, we should keep quiet. This is the standard of behavior devotees should maintain in one another’s association. When devotees gather, it is better (and a higher standard) to say nothing at all if all that can be said is gossip. But we know that after some time, everyone will begin to look at one another and feel uncomfortable. Eventually, someone will give in to the urge to speak. Bhaktivinoda Thakura recommends that we say something connected to Krsna.

When devotees gather and know that they should not discuss *prajalpa,* they wonder who will speak *krsna-katha* first. Sometimes devotees even ask whether the other devotees would want to hear about Krsna. They may be afraid that speaking about Krsna makes them look puffed-up. But the *krsna-katha* has to start flowing somewhere. We should be aware that we are *practitioners.* We are not pure devotees gathering for *sadhu-sanga.* A practitioner is someone who is practicing, who has not yet reached the spontaneous platform. Therefore, he doesn’t feel the spontaneous realization that Krsna is present in his life at every moment. Because he doesn’t have that realization, he will not be able to share it with others. His *krsna-katha* may not have the same impact that a pure devotee’s conversation would have. Nonetheless, he is a practitioner, so let him practice.

This may take common agreement. Can everyone agree that we are practitioners in devotional service? Then we should agree to practice speaking about Krsna. By such practice, what will happen gradually? We will begin to realize the satisfaction that comes from *krsna-katha.* This is why Srila Rupa Gosvami establishes giving and receiving confidences as a basis for exchange between Vaisnavas. The fourth canto also describes that in the presence of a superior, an inferior should be happy and receptive. Actually, the *Bhagavatam* uses the word “jolly”: “Instead of being envious of a more qualified man, one should be jolly to receive him. ” (SB [4.8.34](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-4/8.34), purport) If we are jolly when we receive superior Vaisnavas, we will be able to create an atmosphere that encourages the superior Vaisnava to speak *krsna-katha.*

We can examine how Maharaja Prthu received the four Kumaras. Prthu Maharaja created an atmosphere for the Kumaras to speak. Whenever a more advanced Vaisnava appears in the *Bhagavatam,* immediately the other devotees begin to think of how to create the proper atmosphere. When Suta Gosvami was received at Naimisaranya, he was offered an elevated seat and glorification. Then, the sages asked many relevant questions. The sages asked their questions because they realized that before them was a person qualified to speak. Therefore, they followed proper Vaisnava etiquette by asking a more experienced devotee to speak about Krsna. Suta Gosvami responded by glorifying the sages who were in attendance: “O sages, I have been justly questioned by you. Your questions are worthy because they relate to Lord Krsna and so are of relevance to the world’s welfare. Only questions of this sort are capable of completely satisfying the self. ” (SB [1.2.5](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-1/2.5)) This shows that the sages were successful in creating an atmosphere for *krsna-katha,* an atmosphere of inquiry and confidentiality.

When Vaisnavas appear, devotees should exchange their realizations. In this way, they will become friends with *krsna-katha* as the common basis for dialogue. One devotee can share his realization while another hears attentively, then the other devotee may also speak—either to share his own realization or to inquire to inspire further discussion.

We can practice this. It takes effort. If we want to defeat the urge to speak unnecessarily, and because we are social beings, we must have certain criteria for how to socialize. Devotees should speak only about topics connected to Lord Hari or topics favorable to devotional service. All other topics are counted as giving in to the pushing to speak.

Bhaktivinoda Thakura next discusses the mind. It is the duty of a sober person to tolerate the mind’s demands. Until we are habituated to control the mind’s demands, how can we perform devotional service attentively? A materialist sits in the chariot of the mind and never finds relief from its urgings except in sleep. Even during sleep, the materialist takes shelter in dreams and suffers nightmares. In order to regulate the mind’s demands, *rsis* have recommended that people practice astangaand *raja-yoga.*

But the Lord’s prescription is different. Krsna says that we should regulate the mind by renouncing an inferior taste for a higher taste. Those on the path of devotional service find it easy to regulate the mind. The mind cannot remain without demands. If urged toward spiritual subjects, however, the mind will be properly engaged. Then the mind will not hanker for trivial or low-class engagement.

Many people think that without practicing *astanga-yoga* the mind cannot be controlled. Patanjali Muni, however, has accepted that just as *astanga-yoga* regulates the mind, so *bhakti-yoga* can also regulate it. The meditation on the Supreme Lord propounded by Patanjali is not pure devotional service. Rather it is *sakamabhakti:* motivated devotion. If devotional service is performed for the purpose of regulating the mind, then our devotion is not free of material desires. If we wish to cultivate pure *bhakti,* we must offer our service for Krsna’s pleasure. Whenever we perform pure devotional service, our minds become joyful automatically. This is important to note. Bhaktivinoda Thakura explains that to meditate on the Lord for the purpose of controlling the mind is *sakama-bhakti.* It is *bhakti* motivated by the desire for a peaceful mind. Similarly, if we chant Hare Krsna—the great, mind-freeing mantra—simply to control the mind, we will not attain the pure results. Mental control is simply a by-product of chanting. It comes along with relief from material distress. Relief is not the goal of chanting. When we realize that the uncontrolled mind is causing disturbance, we may be tempted to use *bhakti* to control it, but we will not attain Krsna in that way.

Still, we must learn to control the mind. Krsna states:

**“For him who has conquered the mind, the mind is the best of friends; but for one who has failed to do so, his mind will remain the greatest enemy. ” (Bg.** [**6.6**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/6.6)**)**

When the mind is fixed on a subject matter that we know is not going to satisfy the self and that will be the cause of distress— to ourselves or others—we will suffer. Unproductive mental life is one of the *klesas,* material miseries. *Adhyatmika-klesa* refers to the miseries caused by the body and mind. It is not only physical pain that causes disturbance but mental agony as well. When the mind is uncontrolled, we become anxious. We search for shelter, but find none. The mind has the tendency to go from one object to the next. Srila Prabhupada compared it to a monkey swinging from tree to tree. On each tree, the monkey indulges his senses, then swings to the next tree. The mind swings from object to object, stopping to taste each one. If we identify with the mind, we will never touch reality. Our lives will be hallucinations based on how we perceive enjoyment. We can never be satisfied with a hallucination. When we feel dissatisfied, we renounce that particular illusion to the next and become attached. Then again we renounce and move on, never finding ultimate shelter.

Srila Prabhupada said that chanting the holy name is beyond the mind, beyond intelligence; it is a spiritual sound vibration. If we want to contact Krsna, we have to seek Him beyond mind and intelligence on the power of the holy name. We cannot approach Krsna through our blunt material senses. Krsna can only be understood through the spiritual senses. Those who identify with the mind never contact reality; they live only in illusion.

The living entity may sometimes realize this and decide he wants to control his mind by fixing it on Krsna’s lotus feet. As Bhaktivinoda Thakura explains, the Patanjali system of meditation does just that. It teaches us how to control the mind. But the mind cannot be *completely* controlled until it has actually found shelter at

Krsna’s feet. When the mind is reposed at Krsna’s feet, free from any other motive, all material desires will stop and all impurities will be destroyed.

The shelter of Krsna’s feet is, of course, only available through the practice of pure devotion—when the Lord reveals Himself fully within the mind. Therefore it is described that as we make progress in devotional service, Krsna is sometimes manifest and sometimes not. We may always try to keep Krsna in our mind, but we may be more or less successful depending on the stage we have attained. In the beginning stages, we may get slight glimpses of Krsna (*abhasarupa).* We actually receive that slight glimpse through contact with Krsna’s devotees. By practicing devotional service, engaging the mind favorably in rendering service to Krsna, we will find Krsna’s *darsana* becoming more and more manifest within the mind.

Therefore, Bhaktivinoda Thakura states that the favorable cultivation of devotional service in relation to Krsna is the real meaning of pure *bhakti,* not mental control. Favorable cultivation means to positively accept Krsna as the object of our service. We are not seeking relief from distress. This is not devotional service. We should instead cultivate a constant and favorable desire to serve the Lord.

At present, we may not be able to fix our mind continuously upon Krsna’s lotus feet. Therefore, in *Bhagavad-gita,* Krsna tells us what to do:

**“My dear Arjuna, O winner of wealth, if you cannot fix your mind upon Me without deviation, then follow the regulative principles of** ***bhakti-yoga.*** **In this way develop a desire to attain Me. ” (Bg.** [**12.9**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/12.9)**)**

Krsna understands that it is not easy for us to fix our minds completely upon Him. But if the living entity is always favorably thinking about how to fix his mind while following the regulative principles of devotional service, Krsna will help him. Regulative principles means “regulated. ” A devotee offers Krsna service, choosing to perform those activities that cultivate a favorable relationship with Him. He seeks advancement and therefore meditates on how to improve himself. If we have a particular service to Krsna, if our meditation is to reject that service in order to accept another— because the service doesn’t please us—then that is not favorable to the cultivation of *bhakti.* “Favorable” means that we are doing those things that help increase our service attitude toward Krsna. As soon as we begin to calculate things according to what is favorable for ourselves, then we are no longer striving for pure *bhakti.*

So what *is* favorable for our *bhakti?* How many times we are faced with that question! Answering it often causes devotees anxiety. Certainly every preacher has the experience of having to judge between favorable and unfavorable. For example, we may be preaching to people, but we don’t want to preach in such a way that we make them unfavorable to Krsna consciousness. At the same time, we want to see them advance toward spiritual life. Sometimes we have to preach strongly. A preacher may think, “Should I preach strongly so that this person can come forward and advance? But if I preach *too* strongly, he may turn away. ” We have to search our hearts and also try to see into the heart of the person to whom we are preaching, and by meditating on what will be most favorable for *bhakti,* we have to decide how to act.

We have to make a conscious effort in everything we do to make decisions favorable for Krsna’s service. If we think of Krsna, then we will become attracted to Him as will the people to whom we are preaching. If we are preaching because we want something the other person has to give, we will not really share Krsna. How will that be favorable devotional service? Even if we think we want that thing for Krsna, we will become personally attached to it—or the glory that will come from acquiring it—and we will not have performed pure *bhakti.*

Therefore, devotees should be careful to examine their motivations. If we are motivated by what is favorable for us, we are not striving for pure devotion; if we are motivated by what is favorable for Krsna, then we are moving toward pure *bhakti.* Examining our motives is part of devotional practice. We are meant to practice Krsna consciousness because we have not yet achieved the perfectional stage. Especially in preaching, when we are purely motivated, Krsna consciousness becomes an attractive experience for everyone involved.

Krsna is in the heart, and He can dictate to anyone in any way He likes. In a sense, a devotee doesn’t even have to ask Krsna to fulfill something. Our duty is simply to think about what would be most pleasing to Krsna. What is most pleasing to Krsna is to help others become Krsna conscious. The decision to preach because it is pleasing to Krsna is the best motivation. If we hold this favorable objective in our minds while sharing Krsna consciousness with others, then we cannot fail.

A devotee will always be successful in his attempts to please the Lord because he always thinks of what is favorable for Krsna’s service. Prahlada Maharaja tells his schoolmates: “My dear sons of demons, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, Narayana, is the original Supersoul, the father of all living entities. Consequently there are no impediments to pleasing Him or worshiping Him under any conditions, whether one be a child or an old man. The relationship between the living entities and the Supreme Personality of Godhead is always a fact, and therefore there is no difficulty in pleasing the Lord. ” It is not difficult to please the Lord because He is *bhava-grahi janardana.* He accepts the essence of a devotee’s attitude and does not look at what even might be considered as a fault or mistake. Krsna states that a devotee who works in Krsna consciousness cannot lose. There is never any loss or diminution for those who strive to please Krsna. Actually, the reciprocation with Krsna will be beyond our imagination. Krsna’s acceptance of our service is the perfection of all our endeavors.

Making decisions that are favorable to *bhakti* takes patience, because we cannot always immediately see the results of our activities.

We have to leave the results up to Krsna and remain dependent on Him. If we were able to fully depend upon Krsna in every endeavor, always trying to do what is best for His service, Krsna may choose to arrange things beyond anything we could have expected.

Favorable devotional service is the most important part of the *sadhaka’s* process. Bhaktivinoda Thakura explains, *“SrimadBhagavatam* ([7.1.32](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-7/1.32)) states, *tasmat kenapy upayena manah krsne nivesayet...* Somehow or other one must consider the form of Krsna very seriously. If one follows this instruction, then the mind will be engaged at the lotus feet of Krsna and it will not easily wander into other subjects. For the practitioner the urge of the mind is regulated by pure cultivation of favorable devotional service in relationship to Krsna. By carefully meditating on this subject one can know the inherent differences between yoga and *bhakti. ”*

If we try to understand what he is saying, we will learn to see the difference between favorable meditation on the Lord’s service and yoga practice meant for relief from distress. Such yoga practice is called *sakama-bhakti,* but actual devotional service is known as *akama.* A Krsna *bhakta* is *niskama,* without material desires. Those who are performing yoga meditation, the *muktis* (liberationists) and the *bhuktis* (sense gratifiers) are so filled with lusty desires that they cannot find peace. The Krsna *bhakta* is peaceful because he knows that all desires are fulfilled by Krsna. Instead of striving to fulfill them himself, he engages favorably in Krsna’s service and doesn’t worry about anything.

After hearing this information, devotees often wonder whether the desire to attain Krsna’s lotus feet is a selfish desire. Of course, the answer depends on the motivation behind the desire. Wanting to attain Krsna’s feet can be a pure desire to a mixed desire if our devotional service is mixed with anything other than interest in serving Krsna. For example, if a devotee wants to attain Krsna’s feet in order to get out of the material world, that would be a selfishly motivated desire, not fully spiritual. But Prabhupada always quoted the verse *akamah sarva-kamo va, moksa-kama udara-dhih, tivrena bhakti-yogena, yajeta pumsam param:* “A person who has broader intelligence, whether he be full of all material desire, without any material desire, or desiring liberation, must by all means worship the supreme whole, the Personality of Godhead. ” (SB [2.3.10](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-2/3.10)) So even if our service is tinged with selfishness, that selfishness will be purified.

Nothing is wrong with having a desire to return to the spiritual world. If we make attaining Krsna’s lotus feet our goal, and then do everything motivated by the desire to achieve it, then even if there are still material desires within the heart, they will be gradually spiritualized. Dhruva Maharaja is evidence for this. He wanted to attain the Lord, but when he actually succeeded, he realized that he had attained much more than he expected. He then lost all interest in the material desires that had prompted him to seek out the Lord in the first place.

When we ask about selfishly motivated and pure desires, we have to examine the heart of the person doing the desiring. One person may be completely pure, and another mixed. It depends on the purity of heart. Still, whether we have no desires or are filled with desires, an intelligent person will worship Krsna under all circumstances. That’s why Prabhupada compared the practitioner to an unripe mango. Ultimately, the activities of the *sadhaka* and the *siddha* are the same, but one practice is unripe and the other ripe. We assume that what is unripe will ripen over time. That will surely happen if we fix Krsna as our goal.

Developing pure motivation is not simply a question of gathering knowledge. The sixth canto explains that even though we may become knowledgeable, we may still misidentify ourselves with Krsna. Prabhupada calls the delusion of thinking oneself God the last snare of *maya.*

We can study the conversation between Maharaja Pariksit and Sukadeva Gosvami in the sixth canto. At first, Sukadeva Gosvami recommends atonement for sins, and then coming to the platform of full knowledge. Full knowledge, however, is not sufficient to uproot the weeds of sinful desires. Therefore he concludes:

**“Only a rare person who has adopted complete, unalloyed devotional service to Krsna can uproot the weeds of sinful actions with no possibility that they will revive. He can do this simply by discharging devotional service, just as the sun can immediately dissipate fog by its rays. ” (SB** [**6.1.15**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-6/1.15)**)**

And how do we discharge pure devotional service? Not by our own efforts alone. Pure devotion is given to us by the divine mercy of the Lord’s pure devotees. If we can contact pure devotion by hearing from and serving His pure devotees, even if we ourselves are selfishly motivated, we will become purified by the pure devotee’s grace. We cannot offer anything directly to Krsna. Krsna has arranged to accept our offerings through His pure devotee. By offering even impure things through the pure devotee, the offering (and ourselves) becomes purified. Then Krsna accepts it. Krsna does not accept anything that is offered without love. Krsna tastes only the love in an offering.

Pure devotional service descends through the medium of the Lord’s pure devotees. As Prahlada Maharaja explains, the only way to become free from material contamination is to accept the dust of the lotus feet of a pure Vaisnava upon our body. Only by approaching the Lord and rendering service to Him in this way, through His pure devotee can we become free from material contamination. We call that mercy.

There are exceptional cases where persons achieved devotion through the Lord’s direct mercy, but whether it’s the Lord’s mercy or the mercy of the pure devotee, mercy is required for the development of *bhakti.* Without mercy we have no hope of attaining pure devotional service.

The other part of the formula is that we have to become qualified receptacles for the mercy. We cannot simply wait for it to come, thinking, “Well, I will become pure only by receiving mercy. Some people get it and some people don’t. I’ll wait and see if it will come to me. ” Rather, we must learn where to find mercy. The *Bhagavatam* is full of instructions on this point. For example, Lord Kapiladeva says to Devahuti:

**“O My mother, O virtuous lady, these are the qualities of great devotees who are free from all attachment. You must seek attachment to such holy men, for this counteracts the pernicious effects of material attachment. ” (SB** [**3.25.24**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-3/25.24)**)**

There is no other way to counteract the effects of material desire. We have to seek mercy by hearing the divine instructions on how to please Krsna. By hearing those instructions and acting upon them, we become qualified for further mercy.

The *Bhagavatam* also explains that by serving devotees who are free of vice, we are rendering a great service. By that service, our attraction to hearing and chanting *krsna-katha* will increase. Therefore the *Bhagavatam* states that both hearing and *vaisnavaseva* must be there. Don’t wait for lightning from the sky. If we increase our desire for Krsna consciousness, we will begin to see what we have to do to attain it. We shouldn’t be complacent about this.

When we are in a position to petition the Lord, what should we ask of Him. The science of Krsna consciousness is based on knowing what to ask for. To learn this science, we can examine the examples of the great Vaisnavas. What have the pure Vaisnavas asked for when they were in the Lord’s presence? Prabhupada contrasted them with the many materialists who begged him for material benedictions: “Swamiji, please bless my son to become a famous doctor... ” We should not ask for such blessings. Elevated Vaisnavas ask to hear and chant about Krsna. They ask for devotion. They ask for service. They are not interested in material boons given either by Krsna or His pure devotees. None of these desires enter into their relationship with the Lord. Prabhupada gives the example that when you go before a wealthy man, you shouldn’t ask for something insignificant, because he can give you much more. When Khatvanga Maharaja found that he had only a moment to live and was faced with asking a benediction, he remembered his time in the material world and rejected the idea of asking for anything material. Instead he prayed to remember Krsna at the time of death. If we are similarly interested, we will get Krsna’s mercy.

## Be enthusiastic in practicing bhakti-yoga

**Bhaktyaloka Kishinev, April 14, 1999**

In *Bhaktyaloka*, Bhaktivinoda Thakura speaks about *utsaha*, enthusiasm. Sometimes when we hear the word *enthusiasm* we mistake it for the word *passion.* We think enthusiasm means running around and staying very busy. But that is not the definition given by Rupa Gosvami in his *Upadesamrta or* Bhaktivinoda Thakura in his commentary on that book. Bhaktivinoda Thakura states that without enthusiasm, we begin to neglect our worship. He writes, “Negligence is born of inactivity, apathy, or indifference. Laziness and inertia are called inactivity. When *utsaha* is generated, laziness and inertia cannot remain. ”

We know from the study of physics that Newton used the word *inertia* to describe a body at a state of rest, and its tendency to stay in that state. That is, the body offers resistance to external pushings. When a person is described as inert, it means that although someone may be trying to influence him to grow, he remains resistant. There are many statements in the *Bhagavatam* that explain spiritual inertia. For example, Prahlada Maharaja describes, “Because of their uncontrolled senses, persons too addicted to materialistic life make progress toward hellish conditions and repeatedly chew that which has already been chewed. Their inclinations toward Krsna are never aroused, either by the instructions of others, by their own efforts, or by a combination of both. ” (SB [7.5.30](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-7/5.30)) They are inert. They are given good instructions, but they don’t move and don’t make an effort to improve themselves. They are too attached to their present condition.

Another example: Prabhupada tells the story of the wedding party that rowed all night only to find in the morning that they were in the same place. This is because they had forgotten to pull in the anchor. If we are too much addicted to sense gratification, despite good instructions we will not be able to progress. To give up material attachments takes work. If we are not willing to do that work, then we are lazy and inert, and Bhaktivinoda Thakura says that those two states lead to inactivity.

Enthusiasm is the opposite of inactivity. “When *utsaha* is generated, laziness and inertia cannot remain. ” Enthusiasm is extremely important for those who want to free themselves from inertia. Bhaktivinoda Thakura writes, “The absence of the desire to work is inertia, and this inertia is opposite to the nature of spirit. If one allows this inertia to remain in his body or in his heart, then how can he perform devotional service? ”

Inertia is inactivity, but here Bhaktivinoda Thakura adds that such inactivity is accompanied by the absence of the desire to work. This is contrary to the nature of spirit. It is the spirit soul’s nature to always be active. What fuels that activity? Desire.

Desire can never be separated from the soul. The soul is eternal, unborn, undying, primeval and ever existing. And Krsna adds in the third chapter of *Bhagavad-gita* that the living entity can never stop acting, not even for a moment. Even when we are sleeping, the soul is actively dreaming.

The eternal activity of the soul is desire. This means that the soul is always desiring. When Bhaktivinoda Thakura writes that “inertia is opposite to the nature of spirit, ” he means that if we do not have the desire to do anything, that is contrary to our own nature. If we are inert, we will never be able to make spiritual advancement.

This is because devotional service is based on desire; it is voluntary. Devotional service begins when the soul decides to act for Krsna’s pleasure. If the soul is being forced to act in that way, then we cannot call his actions devotional.

Of course, someone may ask, “If devotional service is voluntary, then why do we have to follow so many rules? Rather, we should be allowed to do what we want. ” But the rules and regulations are meant to provide guidelines by which we can learn to awaken our original desire to please the Lord. At present, that desire is covered by the desire to satisfy the material body. The rules and regulations help us change our habits and restore our original identity and desires. The presence of rules does not minimize the voluntary nature of devotional service. Krsna does not force us to follow them.

According to Rupa Gosvami in his *Bhakti-rasamrta-sindhu*, the first stage of *bhakti* is to voluntarily accept the rules and regulations. This means that we accept the practice of *sadhana-bhakti.* If we don’t come to this preliminary stage, we can never practice real devotional service. Rather, devotional service will remain a mystery because we have not awakened our desire to practice it.

Faith is a key ingredient in awakening spiritual desire. Faith is awakened by hearing about Krsna in the association of devotees.

*nitya-siddha krsna-prema ‘sadhya’ kabhu naya*  
*sravanadi-suddha-citte karaye udaya*

**“Pure love for Krsna is eternally established in the hearts of the living entities. It is not something to be gained from another source. When the heart is purified by hearing and chanting, this love naturally awakens. ”**

The original desire to please Krsna is already within the heart. We are misusing that desire and instead of satisfying Krsna, we are trying to satisfy the material body. We may think we do not lack a desire to work—we work to maintain the body, after all—and perhaps we think that it would better if we were working less hard for our own maintenance.

Along with the desire to work, Bhaktivinoda Thakura discusses apathy. “If one allows this inertia to remain in his body or heart, then how can he perform devotional service? The state of apathy develops from carelessness. ” When we are careless, we become apathetic. One should practice devotional service without deviation. This is instructed in the *Bhagavad-gita* ([6.23](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/6.23)) in the following words:

*tam vidyad duhkha-samyoga-viyogam yoga-samjnitam*  
*sa niscayena yoktavyo yogo ’nirvinna-cetasa*

**“This indeed is actual freedom from all miseries arising from material contact. One should engage oneself in the practice of yoga with determination and faith and not be deviated from the path. ”**

Then Bhaktivinoda Thakura quotes Baladeva Vidyabhusana: “One becomes indifferent to whatever work he feels unqualified for. Devotional service should be performed in this mentality. ” This doesn’t mean we give up work, but it means as follows:

“Regarding *bhakti-yoga,* it is said in the *Srimad-Bhagavatam* ([11.20.7-8](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-11/20.7))... “Among these three paths, *jnana-yoga*, the path of philosophical speculation, is recommended to those who are disgusted with material life and are thus detached from ordinary, fruitive activities. Those who are not disgusted with material life, having many desires yet to fulfill, should seek perfection through the path of karma-yoga. If somehow or other by good fortune one develops faith in hearing and chanting My glories, such a person, being neither very disgusted with nor attached to material life, should achieve perfection through the path of *bhakti-yoga. ”* The purport is this: For those who have attained indifference for material activities but have not yet realized spiritual activities, what can remain in their hearts besides indifference? For them the undifferentiated *brahma-jnana* is the topmost achievement. For those who have not attained indifference for material activities due to lack of spiritual realization, there is no way to cleanse the heart other than karma-yoga. Those who have realized spiritual activities and understood that material activities are insignificant attain indifference for all material activities. They accept some material activities as a support for their spiritual awakening, but they have no attachment for such activities. In proportion to the degree that they discuss spiritual topics in devotion, freedom from material association is awakened as an extraneous fruit. ” (*Bhaktyaloka*, P50)

These points are important. A person may say, “I have no impetus to work. I am indifferent to work, because work means simply gratifying the senses. What is the value of maintaining a body that is the source of so much anxiety and distress? I am disgusted with material life and the material body. ” Sometimes people actually become so frustrated that they lose their impetus to do anything. This doesn’t mean they are spiritually realized. Frustration with material life is not synonymous with spiritual realization. Spiritual desire is the eternal activity of the soul; it is not only about negation of matter. When someone does not have spiritual realization and has lost the impetus to work, Bhaktivinoda Thakura says they have become indifferent.

This indifference rarely lasts long, because the soul wants to be active. Eventually they will engage in activity again. But their state of indifference is what *jnanis* seek. To them, material life is suffering. Therefore, salvation means to cease activity. Actually, their concept of bliss is nonexistence.

Of course, that’s not real bliss. Aside from that, it is impossible for the soul to not exist, because the soul is eternal. Still, he desires not to exist and therefore thinks, “Let me become empty. Let me be liberated into nothingness. I will begin by practicing renunciation of my body and attachments. ” Often such persons perform severe austerities to gain such detachment. Then they desire to merge into the eternity of oneness so that they no longer have to feel.

By becoming one with everything, they no longer have to exist as individuals. *Jnanis* think that by renouncing their existences, they have become free from desire, but actually, salvationism is a material desire. No one can stop desiring.

God is supreme. If we choose not to accept His decisions, that’s our choice. When we misuse our minute independence, we will suffer the consequences. Krsna says, “There is no truth superior to Me. ” He concludes for the *jnanis* that they can never become nothing. It is not His will. Still, He is supreme and can fulfill their desires. If they want to be nothing, He can create a situation by which they can pretend to be nothing. *Ye yatha mam prapadyante:* “As all surrender unto Me, I reward them accordingly. Everyone follows My path in all respects, O son of Prtha. ” If the *jnanis* do not want real eternal life, Krsna does not give it to them.

Lord Brahma prays, “O lotus-eyed Lord! Although nondevotees may perform very severe austerities and penances to achieve the highest position, their intelligence is impure. They fall down from their position of imagined superiority, because they failed to take shelter of Your lotus feet. ” Lord Brahma says *vimukti manina. Manina* means “in the mind. ” *Vimukta manina* means one *thinks* he is liberated, and Krsna allows him that delusion.

Similarly, Krsna allows those in the material world to think of themselves as supreme controllers. It is impossible for there to be so many supreme controllers, but still, each living entity functions under the illusion that he is that one who controls even his own destiny. Even though material nature shows him again, and again that he is *not* the supreme controller, the illusory energy is so powerful that he refuses to see the truth. As soon as he gets a little money or power, he again tries to control.

Neither the *karmi* nor the *jnani* have very permanent positions, because they still have to face their desires. If our desires are not fulfilled, over time we will fall down from whatever position we have attained. Therefore, it is stated, “For those who have attained indifference to material activities but have not yet realized spiritual activities, what can remain in their hearts besides indifference? For them the undifferentiated *brahma-jnana* is the topmost achievement. ” That’s the highest they can attain: impersonal realization.

For those who are not indifferent to material activities “due to a lack of spiritual realization, there is no way to cleanse the heart other than *karma-yoga. ”* These are the *karmis.* They have not yet realized spiritual life, and they have not yet become indifferent to material life. They are filled with unsatisfied desires. If such persons want perfection, they must cleanse their hearts by practicing *karmayoga.*

*Karma-yoga* means to work to satisfy desires, but then to take some of the fruit of the activity and offer it to Krsna.

The next step is as follows: “Those who have realized spiritual activities and understood that material activities are insignificant attain indifference for all material activities. They accept some material activities as a support for their spiritual awakening. But they have no attachment for such activities. In proportion to the degree that they discuss spiritual topics in devotion, freedom from material association is awakened as an extraneous fruit. ”
