Although all of this may sound like a different topic than enthusiasm, Bhaktivinoda Thakura is building a case for the necessity of having enthusiasm in order to practice *bhakti.* “Without enthusiasm it is not possible to perform *bhakti-yoga. ”* We have just read that *bhakti-yogis* are indifferent to material activities. *Sadhakas* have realized spiritual activities, but they “still accept some material activities as a support for furthering their realization and advancement in spiritual life. ”

*Bhakti-yogis* have no attachment to material activities. Krsna says in the sixth chapter of *Bhagavad-gita:*

**“One who is unattached to the fruits of his work and who works as he is obligated is in the renounced order of life, and he is the true mystic, not he who lights no fire and performs no duty. ” (Bg.** [**6.1**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/6.1)**)**

Krsna says we have to work as we are obligated, but without attachment. We work only to support our spiritual activity. For example, we have a material body. We cannot cultivate devotional service without the body. Those who have already attained devotional perfection do not require material bodies, because pure devotional service is rendered completely on the spiritual platform. If someone has to cultivate devotional service, he has to have a material body in order to cultivate it.

Of course, the human body is the best body for such cultivation. One can become enthusiastic to work to maintain the material body as a support for his spiritual awakening. We need the material body in order to hear the topics of Lord Hari and increase our faith in and attachment to the Lord. What better reason is there to maintain the material body than to facilitate our hearing and chanting? The *Bhagavatam* states, “Life’s desires should never be directed toward sense gratification. One should desire only a healthy life, or selfpreservation, since a human being is meant for inquiry about the Absolute Truth. Nothing else should be the goal of one’s works. ”(SB [1.2.10](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-1/2.10)) Are we maintaining the body so that we can live like animals? No, we maintain the body simply because the body is a valuable boat to carry us across the ocean of material existence. We maintain the body in order to support our attempts to increase our faith in Krsna and to become more attached to Him through the process of hearing and chanting.

Therefore, Bhaktivinoda Thakura writes, “In proportion to the degree that they discuss spiritual topics in devotion, then freedom from material association is awakened as an extraneous fruit. ” Detachment is not a separate process. It happens automatically. We detach ourselves from the material energy by hearing about Krsna.

This is what *bhakti-yoga* is. It is hearing about Krsna with faith and devotion. Hearing and chanting are the most powerful forms of *bhakti-yoga.* By this process of *bhakti,* both freedom from material attachment and indifference are automatically achieved in proportion to our attachment to the Lord. This is what the *Bhagavatam* means when it says, *vasudeve bhagavati bhakti-yogah prayojitah:* “By rendering devotional service unto the Personality of Godhead, Sri Krsna, one immediately acquires causeless knowledge and detachment from the world. ” (SB [1.2.7](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-1/2.7))

Our enthusiasm is often awakened by some outside impetus. Srila Prabhupada said, “Purity is the force. ” It is not a stick that will push us but rather a pure example. If we actually hear the message of Krsna consciousness presented purely, we begin to desire *bhakti* to grow in our own hearts. What happens when that desire begins to grow? We decide we want to act for Krsna’s pleasure. Then we simply need direction. But the actual impetus comes from the heart’s inspiration to do something for Krsna. We cannot be forced and be practicing *bhakti.*

Sometimes neophyte devotees with their first taste of Krsna consciousness develop wild schemes of how to spread Krsna consciousness. They think they will take over the world. They imagine being able to get huge buildings and have five hundred book distributors at their command. They dream that they will take over the Parliament. They’ll preach to the president! Then everyone will come to them. Then they begin to look at everyone as an instrument in their scheme. “I have this whole plan in mind! I am going to take over the world! If you surrender to me, I’ll be able to take over the world! Don’t ask questions, just have faith and do what I say! Don’t you understand?! I have a perfect scheme to take over the world! Just help me! Why aren’t you surrendering? Surrender! ” But nothing happens. Such devotees may gather a few fanatical followers for a short time, but they aren’t really preaching with purity, and therefore devotees cannot maintain their impetus to serve. When the preaching is pure, then people will naturally come forward and want to serve Krsna. “Sign me up! Tell me what I can do for Krsna. ” The ability to inspire others comes from purity. Wherever there is real Krsna consciousness, we will find an awakening of spiritual desire.

Desire is the natural and eternal companion of the soul. Therefore, it is natural for every living entity to want to do something. We simply have to steer them in the proper direction, pointing them always toward Krsna. Therefore we have to preach purely. There is no greater force than pure preaching.

## Be careful not to offend Vaisnavas

**SB** [**4.2.21**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-4/2.21) **Moscow, October 27, 1996**

*ya etan martyam uddisya bhagavaty apratidruhi druhyaty ajnah prthag-drstis tattvato vimukho bhavet* “Anyone who has accepted Daksa as the most important personality and neglected Lord Siva because of envy is less intelligent and, because of visualizing in duality, will be bereft of transcendental knowledge. ” (SB [4.2.21](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-4/2.21))

Lord Siva’s principal associate, Nandisvara, is speaking this verse. He was disturbed to see his master offended at Daksa’s sacrifice. The story of the Daksa *yajna* is very instructive, because it helps us understand many important points about Vaisnava behavior. Daksa offended an exalted personality of the caliber of Lord Siva. Daksa actually thought himself to be the most important personality. Therefore, he neglected Lord Siva. Daksa was one of Lord Brahma’s sons. At the time of creation, Lord Brahma produced different progeny. First came the four Kumaras. Lord Brahma asked them to become progenitors, but they refused. They wanted to follow the *nivrtti-marga.* Out of anger, Lord Brahma created Lord Siva. Then he created the various progenitors, such as Angira, Atri, Kratu, and Vasistha, who all appeared from different parts of his body. Daksa was among them, appearing from Lord Brahma’s thumb. (Narada Muni was also one of Brahma’s ten sons, but he took to the path of renunciation.)

The other *prajapatis* accepted the path of fruitive activity. The word *Daksa,* for example, means “expert in the execution of fruitive activities. ” Lord Brahma gave Daksa an engagement appropriate to his nature, and so he remained attached to fruitive activity.

Despite his piety, Daksa didn’t always have the ability to discriminate properly. Here he imagines himself in a position superior to Lord Siva. This is because he was thinking in terms of his body and social position and he was bereft of transcendental knowledge. He saw himself as both the chief of the sacrifice and as Lord Siva’s father-in-law. This, he thought, placed him in a superior position to Lord Siva. Therefore, he treated Lord Siva with insolence.

Prabhupada explains in the purport that Daksa and those who supported him were less intelligent. They saw things in terms of duality. Because they were attached to the results of their fruitive activities, they were unable to appreciate Lord Siva’s exalted qualities. Lord Siva was not attached to fruitive activities; he was not attached to the results of the sacrifice. Lord Siva is actually a great renunciant. He has no attachments to external designations. Therefore, although Lord Siva did not offer Daksa respect, we understand that this was not an offense on his part. He was actually in the superior position. In fact, it is described in the *Bhagavatam* that Lord Siva sees with spiritual vision. He was already offering respect by offering obeisances to the Supersoul within Daksa’s heart, even if he was too absorbed in meditation to offer respect to his body. Lord Siva always meditates on Krsna’s lotus feet. Such a personality’s qualifications are self-evident for those who have the power to discriminate. Such highly exalted souls do not have to promote themselves. Their elevated consciousness is proof of their position. Those who are attached to bodily designations and position think that position establishes superiority, but they are wrong.

We may use position in the Lord’s service. It is the motive that is most important. Are we attached to position or to serving the spiritual master’s order? Daksa was attached to his position. That was his problem. As Prabhupada explains in the purport, “Daksa, Nandisvara said, identified himself with the body like other materialistic persons and was trying to derive all kinds of facilities in relationship with the body. He had excessive attachment for the body and, in relation to the body, with wife, children, home and other such things, which are different from the soul. ” Because of these attachments, he could not discriminate properly.

Those who are attached to the bodily conception of life feel threatened in the presence of those who are of superior consciousness. In a later pastime, Daksa feels threatened by Narada Muni, because Narada had taught his sons to take to the *nivrtti-marga.*

If we are excessively attached to fruitive activities, we must be very careful not to offend Vaisnavas, because Vaisnavas are in a superior consciousness. Just because we see a Vaisnava holding a position doesn’t mean they are attached to fruitive activities. Pundarika Vidyanidhi is an example of this. He was an exalted Vaisnava, but externally he appeared to be attached to wealth. Once, Mukunda brought Gadadhara Pandita to see Pundarika Vidyanidhi. When Gadadhara saw Pundarika’s external characteristics, he began to question him in his mind. Why would an exalted Vaisnava accept such opulence? Pundarika was sitting in an ornate chair with an umbrella over it, and was surrounded by expensive paraphernalia. He was dressed in costly clothing and decorated with expensive jewelry. His hair was styled attractively and he was wearing perfume. Gadadhara Pandita’s experience of great Vaisnavas had been of renunciates. He tried to stop his mind from becoming offensive, but he was finding it difficult. Mukunda could understand Gadadhara’s mind, so he decided to show Gadadhara Pandita the true devotional qualities of Pundarika Vidyanidhi. He recited a verse from the tenth canto of *Bhagavatam* about Krsna delivering Putana. As soon as Pundarika Vidyanidhi heard the verse, he manifested ecstatic symptoms of love of God. His symptoms were uncontrollable, and according to the scripture, they were not artificial tears.

Externally it might appear that a Vaisnava has a position of opulence, but we should understand the heart of such a person. But to understand a Vaisnava’s heart is not easy. Thus we must be careful. We shouldn’t criticize what we cannot understand, and we must learn to discriminate between what is genuine and what is not.

We sometimes see people try to gain position by being critics. They find fault in others and try to gather a following among those who are like minded. He is criticizing, however, only to make himself look better. This verse about Daksa is instructive in this regard: those who support people who do not have the power to discriminate will also be deprived of transcendental knowledge. Such persons are implicated in the offense.

Dhrtarastra is an example of this. Out of attachment to his son, he was offensive toward the Pandavas. He wanted his son to attain the throne, and this would make him emperor. He also offended Vidura, even though Vidura was clearly in a superior position. Duryodhana also offended Vidura, who was both his uncle and an exalted devotee. Dhrtarastra was blinded by his attachments. Not only was he materially blind, he was spiritually blind.

Dhrtarastra was certainly implicated in the destruction of his own dynasty because of his excessive attachment to his son and the support he lent him. Even Lord Krsna came before Dhrtarastra and Duryodhana as Arjuna’s messenger and tried to convince them to see things properly. But he was too attached. He could not accept the Lord’s advice. When Dhrtarastra could not hear from Krsna, it was clear that the Battle of Kuruksetra would have to be fought.

Even Vidura thought, “Because of my association with Dhrtarastra and Duryodhana, I will not be able to achieve Krsna’s lotus feet. ” He too felt implicated—that his association with them had destroyed his pious activities. This is one of the reasons why Vidura decided to leave the palace and travel on pilgrimage: he wanted to purify himself from the association of his brother and nephew.

Vidura was able to realize how he was being implicated, and he chose to both separate and purify himself. Usually, we don’t recognize the implications of our acts.

In this fourth canto verse, Nandisvara curses Daksa and those who supported him to be bereft of transcendental knowledge. Thus they would not be able to understand the Supreme Personality of Godhead. We can see how dangerous lending such support is. Offending Vaisnavas is always done on the bodily platform. That he was on the bodily platform was Daksa’s shortcoming. He was anxious to organize his *yajna* only because he imagined the fruitive benefits he would accrue. That someone is attached to fruitive activities means he is attached to the external benefits that come from the performance of sacrifice. The more fruitive sacrifices one performs, the more power, comfort, and wealth he acquires. Such persons cannot become interested in Krsna. Therefore, Queen Kunti prays, *janmaisvarya-sruta-sribhir edhamana-madah puman, naivarhaty abhidhatum vai tvam akincana-gocaram*: The Lord can only be approached by those who are materially exhausted. Those who are on the path of material progress, trying to improve themselves by opulence, high education, respectable parentage, and bodily beauty cannot approach the Lord with sincere feelings. One who is attached to the performance of fruitive activities cannot place himself in a fully dependent position on the Lord. He is simply too attached to matter.

Unless our attachments are uprooted from the heart, we will not be able to know the Supreme Lord. Only those who are detached from matter can know Krsna. Only those who have a sincere desire in their hearts to serve Him can know Krsna. Certainly Krsna will reward us according to the particular sacrifices we perform. “As they surrender unto Me, I reward them accordingly. ” Those who surrender to Krsna through *karma-kanda* sacrifices are rewarded with fruitive results and with an increase in their attachment to worldly opulence. Those who surrender through the *jnana-kanda* are rewarded differently.

The difficulty with approaching Krsna through *karma-kanda* sacrifices is that although a practitioner may become free of sinful reaction by performing his *yajnas* according to sastric principles, he does not become free of material desires. The *Caitanya-caritamrta* says that the *karma-kandis* will have to be continually bitten by “wasps and drones”: “Consequently the results of this process are compared to wasps and drones. The living entity is bitten by the wasps and drones of fruitive activity and thus suffers in material existence birth after birth. ” (Cc. *Madhya* [20.135](https://vedarama.com/en/books/cc-madhya/20.135), purport)

The particular example is about Sarva-jna, the all-knowing astrologer. Those who dig for treasure on the western side are compared to *karma-kandis.* When they dig in the west, they are attacked by wasps and drones. That is, they are bitten by their desires to achieve opulence and wealth. That is the result of *karma-kanda* worship. The weeds of sinful desire will not be uprooted from the heart.

This is what Nandisvara’s curse implied for Daksa. Of course, later, Daksa became purified of his offense by receiving Lord Siva’s forgiveness. After this incident, which took place during Svayambhuva Manu’s reign, Daksa performed austerities for four *manvantaras.* Then he took birth again during the Caksusa*manvantara. Thus* he became purified. But it was his offense to Lord Siva that caused him to take another body in the material world. This time he appeared in the womb of Marisa, the wife of the Pracetas. Nonetheless, he was severely punished for his offense. We should understand from this section of the *Bhagavatam* the importance of not remaining in the bodily concept of life and especially in not offending Vaisnavas.

Part of that awareness is to be careful not to become envious of others’ spiritual advancement. We should never think ourselves superior. We should certainly not be envious of those who have opulence or followers or any kind of external position. Our position as devotees is established on the strength of our detachment from the material world. As we know, one is detached from the material world to the degree that he is attached to rendering service to Krsna. That kind of detachment is considered superior renunciation. To use our wealth, position, intelligence—whatever Krsna has given us—in His service is a far superior form of renunciation than to reject such things without understanding their relationship with Krsna. Srila Rupa Gosvami advises in his *Bhakti-rasamrta-sindhu:*

*anasaktasya visayan*  
*yatharham upayunjatah*  
*nirbandhah krsna-sambandhe*  
*yuktam vairagyam ucyate*

**“Those who renounce material things without understanding their relationship with the Lord is not as complete in his renunciation as those who utilize everything for His service. ”**

Sometimes we see devotees telling us that they have renounced all position due to their great detachment. They say they don’t need a position for others to recognize their superior qualities. But we have to be careful. When devotees say that, it’s like someone saying they renounce the Brooklyn Bridge. “I don’t need the Brooklyn Bridge to be recognized as wealthy or superior. ” But the Brooklyn Bridge is not his to renounce! We should not promote ourselves as advanced by saying we are renouncing position.

According to the Vaisnava philosophy, we must use whatever position Krsna gives us for the Lord’s service. That is superior renunciation. That one has a position is not an indication that he is attached. Rather, his attachment or detachment is judged by how he uses his position.

We should know how to discriminate. We should look within our hearts to see how attached we are to position or opulence. How much are we promoting ourselves based on falsely renounced positions? If we are not careful, we may find ourselves implicated on the bodily platform and committing Vaisnava *aparadha.* That is the nature of material attachment. Therefore, Lord Siva teaches us here by his example by maintaining his spiritual vision and remaining on the truly superior platform despite all the cursing and counter-cursing.

## We have to teach by example

**SB 7.11.8-12 Dnepropetrovsk, January 1998**

**“These are the general principles to be followed by all human beings: truthfulness, mercy, austerity (observing fasts on certain days of the month), bathing twice a day, tolerance, discrimination between right and wrong, control of the mind, control of the senses, nonviolence, celibacy, charity, reading of scripture, simplicity, satisfaction, rendering service to saintly persons, gradually taking leave of unnecessary engagements, observing the futility of the unnecessary activities of human society, remaining silent and grave and avoiding unnecessary talk, considering whether one is the body or the soul, distributing food equally to all living entities (both men and animals), seeing every soul (especially in the human form) as a part of the Supreme Lord, hearing about the activities and instructions given by the Supreme Personality of Godhead (who is the shelter of the saintly persons), chanting about these activities and instructions, always remembering these activities and instructions, trying to render service, performing worship, offering obeisances, becoming a servant, becoming a friend, and surrendering one’s whole self. O King Yudhisthira, these thirty qualifications must be acquired in the human form of life. Simply by acquiring these qualifications, one can satisfy the Supreme Personality of Godhead. ” (SB** [**7.11.8-12**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-7/11.8)**)**

This is Narada Muni’s instruction to Yudhisthira Maharaja. Yudhisthira Maharaja had asked by which principles of religion could one attain the ultimate goal of life—devotional service. He wanted to hear about the general occupational duties of human society and the system of social and spiritual advancement known as *varnasrama-dharma.* It’s important to understand the question if we wish to understand the answer.

Maharaja Yudhisthira asked about the principles by which we can come to devotional service. Narada Muni has listed thirty principles here. Srila Prabhupada also mentions an interesting point in a purport later in this chapter: “The Krsna Consciousness movement is meant to develop these brahminical qualities. ” (SB [7.11.35](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-7/11.35)) Srila Prabhupada expected the members of the Krsna consciousness movement to embody brahminical qualities and teach society how to become elevated so that everyone can come to the platform of devotional service.

Narada Muni says that these thirty qualifications must be acquired in the human form of life. In other words, they are a summation of proper human behavior. If these are the standards by which we should be judging human society, then we can just imagine what kind of society we live in today. Modern-day society does not come up to the standard of human life. Actually, we are living in a subhuman society.

Sometimes we think that the Krsna consciousness movement is transcendental and that we don’t have to conform to the standards of human morality. That is not what Srila Prabhupada taught. He taught us to approach these qualifications by engaging in *bhaktiyoga,* especially hearing of the Lord’s pastimes and qualities. But Krsna’s pastimes are above human social standards, and perhaps we think that we are too. For example, we can think of the pastime of Murari Gupta. Murari Gupta is completely on the transcendental plane and is one of the Lord’s intimate associates. It’s described that the Lord’s devotees oftentimes lost external consciousness. They became so absorbed in love of God that they became oblivious to social convention. Sometimes in this internal consciousness they appeared to act in strange ways. One time Murari Gupta went home and asked his wife to please bring him food. At the time, Murari Gupta was absorbed in thoughts of Lord Krsna. He had just left Lord Caitanya’s company. Murari Gupta’s wife then brought him rice and ghee, and with his hand, he mixed them together. That seems normal. But after mixing them, in his ecstasy he took the rice and threw it to the ground, saying, “Krsna, eat! Krsna, eat! ” Murari Gupta’s wife understood his ecstasy, so she immediately brought him more rice and ghee, which he then threw on the ground. This was how he offered food to Krsna.

The following morning, Lord Caitanya went to Murari Gupta’s house. Murari Gupta immediately offered the Lord his obeisances. Lord Caitanya then began to speak about Krsna. But Murari Gupta was a physician, so he asked Lord Caitanya why He had come. The Lord told him He had indigestion. “You have indigestion? What did You eat yesterday? ”

Lord Caitanya said, “You rascal! Maybe you forgot what I ate yesterday, but your wife saw everything! Every time she brought you food, you offered it by throwing it on the ground. I had to accept everything you offered Me! I ate all that rice you offered and now I have indigestion! There is only one cure for indigestion: one has to drink large quantities of water. ” Murari Gupta had a cup of water sitting on his table. Lord Caitanya said, “I have come for that water. ” He then grabbed the water and He drank it all.

From the material point of view we may ask why Murari Gupta was throwing *prasadam* on the floor. Isn’t this against Vaisnava principles? *Prasadam* is sacred, and we shouldn’t throw it on the floor. But this is an example to illustrate the transcendental morality that the great devotees follow based on their pure love for the Lord.

We can think of many examples while hearing the Lord’s pastimes. We often hear of the Lord’s associates behaving in ways that seem contrary to social convention. Does that mean we should imitate them? Can we even do what they do, even though we do not share their transcendental consciousness? When great Vaisnavas lose external consciousness, it means they are not conscious of what they are doing. If we imitate them, we tend to hope people are watching us, perhaps thinking we are advanced devotees.

We are meant to be teachers of human society. We are supposed to show the world how to behave like human beings, because most people are behaving like animals. If we wish to have any hope of becoming Vaisnavas, we have to start by becoming human beings. Isn’t that what Srila Prabhupada taught us? Srila Prabhupada taught us at initiation to accept four vows. If we think about it, what is so transcendental about these vows? They are simply principles meant to elevate us to the human status. We agree not to become intoxicated or to eat meat. These things are the standards of human life.

Naturally we also take a vow to chant at least sixteen rounds a day. It’s understood that by chanting our rounds every day, we will gain strength to follow the regulative principles. A person absorbed in chanting the Lord’s holy name will automatically develop good qualities. Of course, those who are already fully absorbed in love of God, such as Lord Caitanya’s intimate associates, already possess all good qualities, even if they sometimes appear to contradict them. But never when such great Vaisnavas act contrary to these principles do they willfully break them. In any case, such persons are above the rules and regulations.

When devotees in Lord Caitanya’s time rolled on the ground to the point that their clothes came off, they were in ecstasy. They were not exhibitionists. They were simply not aware of their surroundings. This is the symptom of the biggest ecstasies in love of God.

We also see that devotees who are situated on the highest platform do not exhibit such symptoms, because they are teaching human society how to act. Very few persons are situated on the transcendental platform. Therefore, these *acaryas* set an example by their own behavior.

But when we hear of the Lord and His devotees sharing transcendental pastimes, we feel pleased. These pastimes cleanse the heart of all dirt. When our heart becomes clean, we will develop the qualities listed by Narada Muni in his instruction to Maharaja Yudhisthira. Possessing these qualities is essential if we hope to teach others how to behave like human beings.

Therefore, we should be careful not to think we are transcendental to regular human standards. We have been given no license to think that we are transcendental to these standards. Narada Muni explains that these qualities are general principles; all human beings must follow them. In his purport, Srila Prabhupada presents the principles in a way that embrace all races and all religions. For example, he defines *svadhyaya,* “reading transcendental literatures, ” as “such literatures as *Bhagavad-gita, Srimad-Bhagavatam* and *Ramayana,* and for those not in Vedic culture, reading of the Bible or Koran. ” These principles are for everyone.

We should also note how clearly Prabhupada defines *brahmacaryam.* He leaves no room for misinterpretation: it is “abstinence from sex life. ” He then describes nonviolence as “not subjecting any living entities to the threefold miseries. ” Each quality is worth studying and discussing.

They are all contained in the *Srimad-Bhagavatam*—the book describing the beautiful pastimes of the Supreme Personality of Godhead. We should understand that hearing the *Bhagavatam’s* message includes hearing the great sages’ instructions on ideal human life.

The first quality listed in this verse is truthfulness. Truthfulness is very important. In the eighth canto, Bali Maharaja states, “There is nothing more sinful than untruthfulness. Because of this, mother earth once said, ‘I can bear any heavy thing except a person who is a liar. ’” A person who willfully lies is sinful.

Prabhupada describes truthfulness in the *Bhagavad-gita* ([10.4-5](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/10.4)): “Truthfulness, means that facts should be presented as they are, for the benefit of others. Facts should not be misrepresented. According to social conventions, it is said that one can speak the truth only when it is palatable to others. But that is not truthfulness. The truth should be spoken in a straightforward way, so that others will understand actually what the facts are. ” Sometimes a person refuses to tell the truth because he thinks the other person won’t like it. Prabhupada insists that the truth must nonetheless be spoken. We are meant to be straightforward in our dealings. We should not hide the truth. When Bali Maharaja made his statement about truthfulness, he had just promised Lord Vamanadeva three steps of land. His guru, Sukracarya, presented reasons why he shouldn’t honor this promise, and Bali Maharaja made this statement: there is nothing more sinful than a liar. How could he not keep his promise? We can learn from him the importance of truthfulness.

Like Bali Maharaja, we should keep our promises. Yudhisthira Maharaja was also known to uphold the truth. He never spoke a lie, and everyone respected him for that. They also trusted him. When a person is truthful, they encourage trust. When people lie, it breeds mistrust. There is so little trust in modern society, because society is full of duplicity and cheating. Prabhupada describes in the first canto that the last leg of religion, truthfulness, is being destroyed by the lying propaganda of cheating politicians. The citizens cannot have faith. Therefore honesty is such an important quality. How will we be able to teach human society to become truthful if we cannot speak the truth ourselves?

Of course, while speaking the truth, we can try to make it palatable. Sometimes that may not be possible. Still, we have to present it. Otherwise, we will not be able to teach others how to be truthful. We must start with being truthful among ourselves. This too is an important point. We are supposed to teach truthfulness to others, but if we cannot practice it among ourselves, how will we accomplish that?

Sometimes to fulfill our material ambitions we speak half-truths. Half-truths are still lies. Half-truths are what we think others want to hear, even though they do not contain the full story. If we speak the whole story, our plans will be ruined, so we leave out whatever will cause us difficulty.

If we want to learn to uphold truth, we must learn to depend on Krsna for the outcome. The Pandavas were true to their word. When Maharaja Yudhisthira lost his wealth, kingdom, brothers, and everything else he possessed to Duryodhana through Sakuni’s cheating, he was left only with his word. He had to give everything to Duryodhana, but because he still had his good word, he fulfilled the obligation. He was completely truthful.

Although Bhima was angry, he couldn’t retaliate even when Duryodhana tried to publicly disrobe Draupadi because Yudhisthira had gambled him away. Bhima felt powerless, but he took a vow at that time to personally kill Duhsasana and to drink his heart’s blood. He also vowed to break Duryodhana’s thighs. He kept his vow when the time came, but he also upheld Yudhisthira’s word, even during the gambling match. Ultimately, he depended on the good will of the Lord for the outcome of his endeavors. All of the Pandavas had firm faith that Krsna was with them and would make everything perfect. Because a devotee is dependent on Krsna, he is able to be truthful and honorable in his dealings.

The second quality listed is mercy. These first four principles— truthfulness, mercy, austerity, and cleanliness—are the four principles of religion. Mercifulness is another essential quality. We should always be merciful toward others. A devotee never thinks of committing violence toward others. This is called *ahimsa,* nonviolence. A*himsa* means “not to inflict the threefold miseries upon any living being. ”

The best way a devotee can show mercy is to distribute the message of Godhead throughout society. In this way, he will always be acting for the enlightenment of human society. A devotee is one who distributes Krsna’s mercy. We often say that the spiritual master is merciful because he distributes the Lord’s mercy just as a raincloud distributes water from the ocean. A rain cloud does not carry its own water, but takes water from the ocean. In the same way, a devotee carries the Lord’s mercy and shares it with others.

Another way we can be merciful is to develop these qualities and then teach them to others. I would like to emphasize this point: we should first acquire these qualities, then teach them to others. Srila Prabhupada says that a person who doesn’t practice what he teaches is a hypocrite; no one will follow him. He also said that example is more important than precept. How can we convince others to be truthful or merciful if we do not exemplify those qualities ourselves? “Whatever actions a great man performs, common men will follow. Whatever standards he sets by his exemplary acts, all the world pursues. ”

We are members of Lord Caitanya’s *sankirtana* movement—a movement intended to distribute Lord Caitanya’s mercy throughout the world. Srila Prabhupada referred to this movement as the most important movement for the enlightenment of human society. We are trying to establish ourselves as people who can be followed. But putting ourselves forward in preaching means we have to be careful how society perceives us—which means we have to be careful how we act. Even a small black spot on a white sheet can be easily seen, what to speak of many black spots.

Lord Narayana told the four Kumaras that a wrong act committed by servants leads people to blame their master, just as a spot of white leprosy on any part of the body spoils the entire skin. Krsna specifically states that a wrong act performed by someone who claims to be a servant will immediately make the people blame the master. We are trying to glorify Srila Prabhupada, but if we don’t behave in the way that Srila Prabhupada expected, then people will blame Srila Prabhupada for our bad behavior. We have to teach by example.

The next quality is austerity. Austerity can be discussed quite thoroughly, but let us say this: human life is meant for austerity. Srila Prabhupada often said that austerity is the wealth of the *brahmanas.* Our austerity is to voluntarily accept inconvenience in order to please Krsna. Pleasing Krsna should be the goal of all our austerities. Whatever we do, whatever we eat, whatever we offer or give away, whatever austerities we perform, should be offered to Krsna.

Austerity for us means voluntary restriction. Srila Prabhupada states that this is the meaning of human life. The difference between humans and animals is that animals cannot restrain themselves. They can only act according to the dictates of their senses. A human being with developed intelligence will voluntarily restrain his senses. Included in Prabhupada’s description of austerity is “observing fast on certain days of the month, like Ekadasi, and festival days like Janmastami, Gaura-purnima, etc. ” We fast in order to increase our remembrance of Krsna. That is the meaning of austerity. “Lord Rsabhadeva told His sons: My dear boys, of all the living entities who have accepted material bodies in this world, one who has been awarded this human form should not work hard day and night simply for sense gratification, which is available even for dogs and hogs that eat stool. One should engage in penance and austerity to attain the divine position of devotional service. By such activity, one’s heart is purified, and when one attains this position, he attains eternal, blissful life, which is transcendental to material happiness and which continues forever. ” (SB [5.5.1](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-5/5.1))

The next quality is cleanliness. “Cleanliness means bathing twice a day and remembering to chant the holy name of God. ” We are meant to keep ourselves internally and externally clean.

*om apavitrah pavitro va sarvavastham gato ’pi va yah smaret pundarikaksam sa bahyabhyantara-sucih*

**“Whether impure or pure, or having passed through all different conditions of life, if one remembers the lotus-eyed Krsna he becomes both internally and externally clean. ”** ***(Garuda Purana)***

It’s not that we can simply depend upon our external cleanliness by remembering Krsna. We shouldn’t think, “I’ll remember Krsna today and skip the bath. ” Remembering Krsna means remembering that we are supposed to bathe twice in a day. We remember the deity, and therefore we don’t go before the deity in an unclean state. Remembering Krsna helps us to become clean internally and externally.

Sometimes devotees consider it an austerity to bathe twice a day. Prabhupada says that this is not austerity but hygiene. Hygiene is important to human life. If it’s austere for us to bathe twice in a day, we should accept the austerity. It is important to keep the body clean. Prabhupada even quoted in one letter, “Cleanliness is next to godliness. ” Cleanliness is another aspect of brahminical life.

Aside from personal cleanliness, Prabhupada said a *brahmana* should always leave a place cleaner than he found it. He should clean up after himself, and not be afraid to clean up after others. He certainly shouldn’t leave his mess for others to clean up. Sometimes we have seen devotees who think like this: “I’m a book distributor, let the less important dishwashers wash my plate. ” We should *all* clean.

Prabhupada set standards for us. He told us to always clean the place where we have eaten. In ISKCON’s early days, there was no question of taking *prasadam* without a bucket of water. No one would leave the *prasadam* room without having wiped his or her place.

Similarly, there are ways to be clean with our hands. We should never touch something dirty, then something sacred. That’s why I am always asking the devotees not to fill up the water cup for the *Bhagavatam* class all the way. If it’s filled to the top, how can I pour it? I can’t drink and then touch the *Bhagavatam.* We shouldn’t drink or eat anything and then touch something sacred. That’s part of cleanliness.

We should try to be conscious of these standards of cleanliness. If we touch something, drink something, and want to chant on our beads, we should first wash our hands. We should never touch something as sacred as our beads with unclean hands. We should also always bathe after becoming contaminated, such as after evacuation. Sometimes that may be difficult, but such situations should be rare.

The next quality is *titiksa,* tolerance. There are many verses describing tolerance in the scripture. *Bhagavad-gita* ([2.14](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/2.14)) states: *matra-sparsas tu kaunteya*, *sitosna-sukha-duhkha-dah agamapayino ’nityas, tams titiksasva bharata* “The non-permanent appearance of happiness and distress and their disappearance in due course, are like the appearance and disappearance of winter and summer seasons. They arise from sense perception, O scion of Bharata, and one must learn to tolerate them without being disturbed. ”

Tolerance is an important quality. A devotee must learn to tolerate all kinds of obstacles and remain determined to execute his duties for Krsna’s pleasure.

There is so much we could say on each of these thirty qualities. At least we should study them and become more conscious of trying to apply them in our lives. Although hearing *Srimad-Bhagavatam* class means hearing the Lord’s pastimes, we also want to hear the instructions of the great sages for our own benefit.

## Relationship Between Devotees

## We must strive for cooperation and unity

**Ista-gosthi**

**Boston, April 1995**

Real success is our willingness to voluntarily sacrifice for the sake of preaching Krsna consciousness. This creates unity and inspires us to work for our own spiritual well-being. In Krsna consciousness there is no disunity. Srila Prabhupada writes in his purport to *Srimad-Bhagavatam* [4.13.9](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-4/13.9):

“Since the sons of King Pracinabarhisat were all united in Krsna consciousness, the Lord was very pleased with them. The unity of the individual souls attempting to satisfy the Supreme Lord or rendering service to the Lord is real unity. In the material world such unity is not possible. Disunity between individual souls is so strong within this material world that even in a society of Krsna consciousness, members sometimes appear disunited due to their having different opinions and leaning toward material things. Actually, in Krsna consciousness there cannot be two opinions. There is only one goal: to serve Krsna to one’s best ability. ”

If we wish to spread Krsna consciousness, we must make a unified effort for a unified goal. In order to do so, we must be committed and willing to sacrifice. We already have the ability to live, eat, and sleep peacefully in the temple. Prabhupada states, “If that is the success, then that success they have got and this was condemned by my Guru Maharaja—to earn some money by showing the Deity in the temple. Eat and sleep. Better you become a sweeper in the street to earn your honest livelihood and live. Many temples in Vrndavana have the same mentality. They think the operation of the temple is a business, that people are coming and giving something—that is their income—and they think this is their success. So the temple is deteriorating. Success is he who is pushing forward the preaching movement. If we think by showing the Deity and getting some money and living peacefully—that much success they have got. ”

We are not successful simply by “showing the Deity” and competing among ourselves to get more people to attend our temple (and give us money) so we can afford to have a better standard of eating and sleeping. This is not the success Lord Caitanya’s mission promises us. Srila Prabhupada always emphasized that Lord Caitanya wanted to spread Krsna consciousness—the teachings of *Bhagavad-gita* and *Srimad-Bhagavatam*—to everyone. To fulfill this mission, we have to fulfill the responsibilities we have been given.

Everyone must think in a unified way to see how we can contribute. If everyone has a personal agenda and cannot think about the overall unity, we will never find the nectar for which we are always hankering. We will find only a comfortable place to eat and sleep. The nectar in temple life is found when the temple is vibrant with the preaching spirit. If we think, “Let me take part in *that* unified effort, ” then we will feel the excitement of working toward a common and transcendental goal. If we think, “I have my own agenda and can take or leave the preaching mission, ” then there is no way to access the nectar. Such a devotee will not feel a part of the community spirit.

If we want to sustain ourselves in spiritual life, we cannot think only of ourselves. The way to sustain ourselves in spiritual life is to think how to care for others. When we care for others, Krsna takes care of us. When we think we will take care only of ourselves, we don’t feel cared for. Real sustenance comes when Krsna takes care of us.

Krsna notices the devotees who sacrifice to serve Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s mission. That mission is meant to expand the Lord’s glories and to care for others in Krsna consciousness. When a devotee asks, “How can I take part in the effort to expand the Lord’s glories? What sacrifices can I make? ” then Krsna cares for him and he feels sustained.

If we want to be sustained in spiritual life, we simply have to *take part.* Krsna consciousness is a joyful process. We can’t think only of how to maintain temples, but how to expand the preaching. If we misuse the temple for personal maintenance, the temple will deteriorate; if we use it to expand the preaching, Lord Caitanya will sustain it.

When we participate in the preaching mission, we feel whole. Devotees sometimes mention that they feel incomplete and they begin to look for something to fill in the hole. What makes us complete, however, is our full connection to the Supreme Lord. Krsna is the complete whole, and when we are connected to Him fully, we will feel complete in ourselves. Serving Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s preaching mission helps us feel the connection. We should be anxious to know what we can do to serve the preaching.

How can we serve the mission? We may not know. We must accept the preaching order as it comes down to us in disciplic succession. We shouldn’t think of negotiating a contract with Lord Caitanya. We should simply want to know His order and then try to carry it out. If we work in this way, we will feel an integral part of the overall effort, we will no longer have to worry about the temple being maintained—it will maintain itself—and we will advance in spiritual life. None of this can be accomplished by material formulas. Srila Prabhupada said, “Material formulas and spiritual life do not go hand in hand. ” We must apply spiritual principles, by Lord Caitanya’s mercy.

We need to understand that the vision for creating unity in the temple and working to fulfill that vision is not mundane. All the members of the community should share the desire to spread Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s mercy. Everyone in the community should be willing to take part in the sacrifice required to bring that about. Then each devotee will feel the dynamic of spiritual life. Caitanya Mahaprabhu made devotional service accessible, and this is *how* it’s accessible. If we try to practice Krsna consciousness in our own way, then we will be like fools trying to touch the moon with our fingers. The moon is not so easily reached. Krsna consciousness is reached when we fulfill the orders coming from authority and work in a unified way.

## How are loving relationships developed among devotees?

Loving relationships are developed when we care for one another. When a devotee sees another devotee caring for him, he naturally feels love for that devotee. In this way, loving relationships are expanded.

That means we have to recognize the care others are giving us. If we are too self-centered to appreciate someone’s love, association, and encouragement, then the love cannot expand.

We experience true love when Krsna is placed in the center of a relationship. If everyone keeps themselves as the center, they will feel an overwhelming degree of impersonalism. They will have their orbit and they will not want anyone to penetrate it. Then there is no love, only tension, as each of us thinks that we have what we need to maintain ourselves and our families, and if anything comes to disrupt that security, we will push it out of our way. How is that love? It’s impersonalism.

Loving relationships develop when there is an effort to do something collectively to please Krsna. The catalyst for such unified action is hearing. The more we hear, the more we will understand the Lord’s messages and the more we will realize how important it is for us to make a personal sacrifice to achieve His goals. Therefore, love begins when the devotees hear and chant together. That creates loving relationships—these exchanges in spiritual life. Love is not based on sentimentality. Loving relationships are formed when we place Krsna in the center—not sentimentally, but with intelligence.

How did Lord Caitanya freely distribute such love? How did He manifest love? How did He make this love so accessible to us? He taught us to freely distribute the holy name, and to practice *sravanam kirtanam visnoh smaranam.* These activities form the loving exchanges between devotees. We expand the love by practicing them and sharing them.

## The Sunday Feast is one of our most important preaching programs

**Sunday Feast Announcement before Lecture Moscow, May 1995**

I'd like to make a short announcement before I give the lecture. I have been hearing over the course of the past week that there is a lack of enthusiasm to advertise our Sunday Feast program. Therefore I wanted to take advantage of this opportunity to mention my concern and to impress upon the devotees that the Sunday Feast is one of our most important preaching programs. It was established by Srila Prabhupada at the very beginning of the movement, and as you know, Srila Prabhupada took part fully in it. He personally cooked all the preparations, then served devotees and guests himself. He also chose to call it the “Sunday Love Feast”. He saw the Sunday Feast as an opportunity to make Krsna consciousness more available to those who had been introduced to Krsna consciousness but who had not become full-time devotees. Prabhupada used the Sunday Feast to encourage and inspire many persons to become Krsna conscious. This should not now become “someone else’s” program or “someone else’s” duty. Just as we emphasize that book distribution is at the forefront of our preaching and that everyone in the temple must see themselves in relationship to the book distribution mission, so we should see that the fruit of book distribution is the attendance at the Sunday Feast. The Sunday Feast should become the vehicle to help people enter deeper into an understanding of loving exchanges between devotees. When the devotees experience loving exchanges, it becomes very inspiring for others.

I witnessed this personally just a few days ago when Krsna inspired me to participate in the Nrsimha Festival in St. Petersburg. I hadn’t been planning to go, but I had a dream in which I was sitting with Bhakti Bhrnga Govinda Maharaja and Indradyumna Maharaja and honoring *prasadam.* I relished their company so much in a dream that I felt Krsna was telling me to go to St. Petersburg to be with them. Two days later, there I was, sitting and honoring *prasadam* with Indradyumna Maharaja and Bhakti Bhrnga Govinda Maharaja. We had such relishable exchanges that I felt ecstasy. It was Indradyumna Maharaja’s Vyasa-puja, and it was wonderful to sit there and listen to all the praises of my godbrother. Then I got a phone call from Mukunda Maharaja who was in the United States. The phone call made me anxious, because I didn’t want to leave the room where Indradyumna Maharaja was being praised. At the same time, I didn’t want to miss out on Mukunda Maharaja’s association. I was torn.

I ended up leaving the room and speaking to Mukunda Maharaja for some time. Then I returned immediately to the presence of the St. Petersburg devotees and my godbrothers. I had been experiencing such wonderful loving exchanges with my godbrothers that I felt like a sense-gratifier. I couldn’t help but enjoy their association. All the other devotees told us afterwards that it was wonderful to see us exchange. Everyone who saw us said the same thing and all of the devotees present felt the love expand among themselves too.

This sharing is what the Sunday Love Feast is all about. Srila Prabhupada started the Sunday Love Feast just to show those who were being introduced to Krsna consciousness what it is to relish the association of devotees. All the devotees should participate in the Sunday Feast, and they should encourage others to participate as well.

The other day a devotee asked how we could advertise the Sunday Feast. I thought about it, and wondered how the Sunday Feast is advertised in other places. There are always new people coming to the Sunday Feast in the American temples, and preachers from all over relish attending the Boston temple’s feast because it is such a wonderful opportunity to preach. I realized that the advertisement is simple: it is our preachers. The best advertisement is our preachers. Devotees go out every day to distribute books. They meet people, and preach in colleges or at different persons’ homes. This is the best way to advertise the Sunday Love Feast. The book distributors should think of how they can encourage others to participate in the Sunday Feast.

But now I have heard the argument that the book distributors think inviting people to the Sunday Feast is a distraction, and if I hadn’t gone out and distributed books for as many years as I did, I might believe them. However, it is impossible to convince me of this argument. No one can convince me that inviting people to the Sunday Feast is a distraction, because I personally did nothing but distribute Prabhupada’s books every day for years and I know better. I could not think of going out on book distribution without my pack of invitation cards. I had these cards with me at all times. Just as it was important that I had my books and my book bag, it was important to have the invitation cards. I felt naked without them.

As far as being a distraction, I just can’t imagine how distributing invitation cards can possibly distract a preacher from preaching. When a preacher gives someone a book, he can also give an invitation card. Even if someone doesn’t take a book, they can still receive an invitation card. How is offering a card a distraction? It is inconceivable! Is there any book distributor here who thinks that inviting people to the Sunday Feast is a distraction? I would like to hear your arguments. It may be that you do have a good argument, something I haven’t yet thought about, so please let me know what it is. I'm willing to be defeated by your argument. Is there any book distributor in the audience who would like to defeat me?

Anyway, I'm encouraging the preachers to please become our best advertisers for the Sunday Feast. If you are preaching to someone, give them an invitation card. In fact, we are all preachers. Even if you are not a book distributor but are doing some other service, you can still distribute invitation cards. I remember that distributing invitations to the Sunday Feast was something Jayananda Prabhu was noted for. Wherever he went, he invited people to the Sunday Feast and to Ratha-yatra. He was always preaching to people. Even though he wasn’t going out on book distribution, he went out to buy *bhoga* or do other chores and he invited people to the Sunday Feast.

Wherever *any* of the devotees go, they should always have invitation cards with them. Whomever you meet—this is Lord Caitanya’s instruction—you should tell them about Krsna. If you don’t want to tell them about Krsna, at least give them an invitation to the Sunday Feast and let the devotees at the Sunday Feast tell them about Krsna.

We have the most powerful vehicle for pushing forward our Sunday Feast right in the lives of these devotees sitting in this room. There should be no question of new people not coming to the Sunday Feast. Then of course, it is also the duty of the devotees organizing the Sunday Feast to make sure new people return. We have to inject in them a good taste of Vaisnava *sanga.* We should always be thinking of how to make our Sunday Feast a relishable experience for others.

## Spiritual Life of Grhasthas

## Hearing and chanting about Krsna are the important foundations of spiritual life

**Bg. 13.26 Nikolaev, June 1996**

**“Again there are those who, although not conversant in spiritual knowledge, begin to worship the Supreme Person upon hearing about Him from others. Because of their tendency to hear from authorities, they also transcend the path of birth and death. ” (Bg.** [**13.26**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/13.26)**)**

In his purport to *Bhagavad-gita* ([13.26](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/13.26)), Srila Prabhupada writes, “Lord Caitanya has said that in this age no one needs to change his position, but one should give up the endeavor to understand the Absolute Truth by speculative reasoning. One should learn to become the servant of those who are in knowledge of the Supreme Lord. ”

All through the *Bhagavad-gita,* Krsna assures us that whatever condition we have attained in life does not need to be changed if we wish to practice devotional service. What we need to do is to learn how to work in the proper consciousness. Krsna explains in the sixth chapter, “One who is unattached to the fruits of his work and who works as he is obligated is in the renounced order of life, and he is the true mystic, not he who lights no fire and performs no duty. ” (Bg. [6.1](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/6.1))

We each must learn how to work. To work properly, we have to change our consciousness. Work is not meant simply to maintain the material body but as an offering to Krsna. Through work, we learn to place Krsna in the center of our lives. If we want to place Krsna in the center of our lives, then we must hear and chant regularly. The *Bhagavatam* states:

*nasta-prayesv abhadresu, nityam bhagavata-sevaya*  
*bhagavaty uttama-sloke, bhaktir bhavati naisthiki*

**“By regular attendance in classes on the** ***Bhagavatam*** **and by rendering of service to the pure devotee, all that is troublesome to the heart is almost completely destroyed, and loving service unto the Personality of Godhead, who is praised with transcendental songs, is established as an irrevocable fact. ” (SB** [**1.2.18**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-1/2.18)**)**

It is essential to regularly hear the *Bhagavatam.* We must hear the *Bhagavatam* every day. If we don’t, we will forget why we are working. We will think only that we are working to maintain the body and its extensions—family, society, and nation. We will not remember that the goal of all work is to give pleasure to Krsna. By working in this consciousness, we increase our attachment to Krsna. The *Bhagavatam* explains, “The occupational activities a man performs according to his own position are only so much useless labor if they do not provoke attraction for the message of the Personality of Godhead. ” (SB [1.2.8](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-1/2.8))

If we are householders, we must both work and regularly hear about Krsna in the association of devotees. This will give us strength. Without this spiritual strength, we will find it easy to forget Krsna. Hearing and chanting about Krsna are the important foundations of spiritual life. Those who practice these two items of *sadhana* seriously will be able to cultivate the faith required to make spiritual advancement.

The guru emphasizes hearing and chanting over everything else for this reason. Everything, including the understanding of the chanting, is contained in the hearing process. It’s a fact that not everyone can give up their work and move into the *asrama* to live renounced lives. The *asrama* is for people who want to live simply, to be celibate, and who wish to become menial servants of the guru. Everyone else lives as a *grhastha.*

While *brahmacaris* live in an *asrama*, so do *grhasthas.* The word *asrama* means “shelter. ” What is the shelter in the *grhastha-asrama?* Narada Muni describes it to Maharaja Yudhisthira in the seventh canto of the *Bhagavatam:* the daily hearing of the Lord’s pastimes.

There is no other shelter in the *grhastha-asrama.* Taking shelter anywhere else means that the *grhastha-asrama* transforms into the *grhamedi-asrama.*

We use the words *grhamedi-asrama* loosely, because they present a paradox. There *is* no *asrama* for *grhamedis,* because they have no shelter, no *asrama.* If *grhasthas* are not hearing the *SrimadBhagavatam* every day, either by attending the temple *Bhagavatam* class or somehow in the association of other devotees, then they are not within the *asrama.*

It is good if the *grhasthas* can come to the temple in the morning. There may be rare circumstances where someone cannot attend the temple to hear, but everyone should mold his or her life in such a way that temple attendance is possible. If devotees can’t come every day, then they should attend at least several days a week. That does not mean that they shouldn’t hear on the other days; they must also work out a lifestyle that allows quality hearing at home. Otherwise, where will they get their strength?

How can the guru help his disciples advance in spiritual life if they are unwilling to hear regularly? It is not initiation that brings a disciple back to Godhead. The connection between guru and disciple is based upon how well the disciple hears and acts upon the spiritual master’s orders. When hearing is submissive and regular, the connection between guru and disciple will be strong.

We often use the example of electric wire being connected to the power source. If we remove the insulation from two wires and tape them together, then connect them to the source of power, they will carry electricity. Sometimes wires appear to be connected, but the copper is not touching inside. In that case, electricity will not flow through both wires. In the same way, we may call ourselves disciples, but if we are not following the guru’s instructions strictly, then the wires are not touching and no Krsna consciousness comes through. It is not initiation that connects us to the *parampara* but the process of hearing submissively and regularly. Initiation is an automatic by-product of the hearing process; it should follow after a connection has been established through hearing.

## What is “ideal family life”?

**SB 7.14.3-4 Riga, July 2, 1997**

**“A** ***grhastha*** **must associate again and again with saintly persons, and with great respect he must hear the nectar of the activities of the Supreme Lord and His incarnations as these activities are described in** ***Srimad-Bhagavatam*** **and other** ***Puranas.*** **Thus one should gradually become detached from affection for his wife and children, exactly like a man awakening from a dream. ” (SB** [**7.14.3-4**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-7/14.3)**)**

This verse was spoken by Narada Muni to King Yudhisthira and recorded in the *Bhagavatam* chapter entitled “Ideal Family Life. ” I smile when I remember that I lectured on this chapter elsewhere and the householder sitting next to me was trying to understand the meaning of ideal family life. He couldn’t understand how the words “ideal” and “family life” went together. In his mind, family life was troublesome. While that may be true, we should note that this chapter was spoken by a *brahmacari* to a householder. Obviously, there must be substance to the combination of words, “ideal” and “family life”. If there was no substance, Narada Muni would not have bothered to discuss it.

Narada was speaking to Yudhisthira Maharaja because Yudhisthira Maharaja asked him to explain how householders, staying at home without knowledge of life’s goal, may easily attain liberation according to Vedic instruction. Narada explained that householders must earn a livelihood, but instead of enjoying the results of their work themselves, should offer those results to Krsna. How we can use the results of our work to satisfy Krsna can be understood perfectly in the association of advanced devotees. That was Narada Muni’s first response to the question. A householder is someone who holds a house. This implies that he has many responsibilities besides those that lead him toward his spiritual goals. Most people “hold a house” because they have a family. A man’s duty toward his family is to provide the family members with life’s necessities. Thus Narada Muni acknowledges that a householder must earn a livelihood. But instead of trying to enjoy the fruits of work himself, a householder should learn to offer the results to Krsna.

Along with family responsibilities, householders usually have social obligations too. Sometimes they become entangled in the network of obligation because it is so entwined with earning his livelihood. As soon as someone has to work for others, they become dependent upon those others for their lives. All householders, in order to maintain themselves and their families, must do some sort of work in society. Human society is designed in such a way that all work contributes to the social whole.

This is also true of the Vedic system. Householders are maintained by the service they render society. The *brahmanas* serve society by studying and teaching the *Vedas,* worshipping the deities and teaching others how to worship Them, and accepting and distributing charity. If a householder cannot render service to society by teaching Vedic knowledge, he may render service by performing administration. An administrator renders service by helping organize others. When society is organized properly, human endeavor can be directed toward the Lord’s satisfaction and at the same time fulfill the economic needs of all concerned.

*Vaisya* householders perform mercantile work or farming, and these too are services performed for the social good. The merchants are responsible for the buying and distribution of goods and food. Without them, the other castes could not live. Modern *Vaisyas* are dependent on society for the sale of their goods and therefore their economic well-being.

All of these services are interdependent and make a householder dependent on society for his maintenance. Therefore a householder may have to develop friendships that are conducive to his work. Friends may help us by lending us money or performing other services, and we become obliged to reciprocate.

Aside from friendships and similar social obligations, most modern-day devotees do not work for themselves but for employers. Not only do they have to fulfill social obligations, but they must satisfy their employers. Their minds fill with these obligations— they worry about how they will pay the electric bill, the water bill, repay their friend, and increase their production at work so the boss will be satisfied. They may worry about how they will pay their taxes (or avoid paying them), and how they will pay for their children’s education. It is impossible for householders with families to avoid these obligations. It seems to them that everyone but themselves has a claim on the fruits of their work, and when it comes time for them to collect their paycheck, there is a long line of entities waiting to be satisfied. When we speak of life’s necessities, we are speaking about food, shelter and clothing. These needs are also standing in line before we can calculate what is left over.

In the midst of all this, the householder is enjoined to think that he is performing his work for Krsna’s pleasure and that the fruits of his work belong to Him. Narada Muni tells Yudhisthira that he must offer the results of his work to Krsna. How can he do so when so many entities are laying claim to the same fruits?

It is very difficult to understand how the fruits of our work are offered to Krsna in such a circumstance. It is difficult even to think of Krsna under such circumstances. Therefore, Narada Muni provides the key: the *grhastha* must associate again and again with saintly persons, and with great respect, hear of the nectarean activities of the Supreme Lord and His incarnations as they are described in *Srimad-Bhagavatam* and other *Puranas.* Thus one should gradually become detached from affection for wife and children exactly as a man awakens from a dream.

What is Narada Muni trying to convey to King Yudhisthira? He is telling him that one who works for fruitive results is like a man who is dreaming. In his dream, he is working hard to maintain something that can never be real; it *appears* real, but it is so temporary that it can only be an illusion. A person who feels the pressure of his obligations and who works only to satisfy them may think his struggle is the essence of real life, but he is dreaming. Narada Muni tells King Yudhisthira that he must learn to awaken from such a dream by hearing the Lord’s transcendental pastimes.

In the fourth canto, Narada Muni tells King Pracinabarhisat that he should not try to counteract the effects of fruitive activities by manufacturing more activities devoid of Krsna consciousness. To do so is simply ignorance. Narada again uses the example of a dreaming man: a man suffering a nightmare cannot be saved by a bad hallucination. The only way to counteract the effects of a nightmare is to wake up. If we want to counteract the bad effects of material illusion, we have to wake up by practicing Krsna consciousness.

Srila Prabhupada explains that when we form society, friendship, and love in dreams, they disappear the moment we awaken. Similarly, our apparently “real” society, friendship, and love are an illusion from which we can recover. If we do not recover by awakening into Krsna consciousness, we will lose this illusion when we die. Therefore, whether our dreams are subtle or gross, both are false and temporary. When a person awakens from a bad dream, he thinks his nightmare has been relieved. Perhaps he was crying out, perspiring, running fearfully in his sleep. Now he feels he has been awakened to reality. Srila Prabhupada is saying that he has simply awakened from one illusion into another. This other illusion is his sense that his home, family, and possessions that surround him are his and are creating his comfort. They are his shelter. But they are temporary, and it is only a matter of time before death awakens him to that fact.

Why does Narada Muni say this to King Pracinabarhisat? Because the king was using fruitive work to relieve the reactions he was experiencing from his past fruitive work. A person who tries to enjoy the results of past fruitive work must create more reactions. In the end, he suffers because such work creates obligation, which forces him to work and deprives him of his enjoyment. This goes on life after life.

We see in modern society that people are suffering from this cycle. They are forced to work by their desires to enjoy matter. They work hard, and then must give a large portion of the fruits of their work to the bill collectors. Because people know the bill collectors are there, they feel anxious and pressured to work harder. They want to make more money so that they will have something left. But the harder they work and the more they try to consequently enjoy, the larger the payment to the bill collectors.

This goes on not only in this life but in the next, and in each subsequent life, people continue to suffer the reactions for their past activities and desires. Sometimes they commit sinful activities in order to get ahead. In a subsequent life they may suffer poverty as a result. Poverty is antithetical to their goal of enjoyment, but that is all they have earned. Sometimes they suffer disease, legal implications, or from any of a variety of sinful reactions. Again they try to relieve their suffering by performing fruitive work, and so the cycle continues. The only escape is to awaken.

To awaken, we must practice Krsna consciousness. If anyone wants to actually solve the problems of life, he must practice Krsna consciousness. Without Krsna consciousness, the material world remains sometimes a dream and sometimes a hallucination, and under the influence of such illusion we continue to work hard to maintain it. Our night dreams will be short and our day-dreams longer, but we will never meet reality.

When a living entity is taken to the hellish planets, he thinks he suffers for millions of years, but actually he suffers only a few moments. We often feel that our lives are long, but actually they are quite short. We think, in our dreaming state, that we can live until we are ninety years old, but we don’t realize how quickly those ninety years pass. In his song, *Bhajahu Re Mana,* Govinda dasa compares this life to a drop of water on a lotus leaf. “Wealth, sons, wife, youth, family—what do they mean? Just as a drop of water appears on a lotus leaf and can roll off at any moment, so is this life. Therefore let me worship Sri Hari and become Krsna conscious, which is the only necessity. ”

We should believe Govinda dasa’s words: Krsna consciousness is the only necessity. We often hear the phrase, “life’s necessities, ” and we think it means food, clothing, and shelter. These things are for physical maintenance only. The real necessity is Krsna consciousness.

Isn’t it a basic philosophical point of Krsna consciousness that life exists without the body? Why then would we consider the only necessity of life to mean that which keeps life *in* the body? Although Narada Muni tells us that we should work to maintain body and soul together, life has another necessity. That understanding is the underlying principle of Krsna consciousness. We are meant to serve Krsna.

Therefore, it is necessary for us to always remember Krsna, to worship Him, and chant His holy name. Krsna presents this conclusion to Arjuna in *Bhagavad-gita:* “Always think of Me, become My devotee, worship Me and offer your homage unto Me. Thus you will come to Me without fail. I promise you this because you are My very dear friend. ”

Krsna wants us to remember Him through all our activities. He wants us to always work under His protection without fear. If we are conscious of Krsna as we fulfill our material obligations, then all obstacles in life will be overcome. If we act out of false ego and for fruitive gain, however, we will be “lost. ”

Srila Prabhupada explains that everyone, especially the householders who do not know the goal of life, should be given an opportunity to hear about Krsna. Prabhupada uses the word *mudhadhi* for *grhastha*, because householders are usually addicted to sinful activity. He specifically says that sometimes we see *grhasthas* working eightor ten-hour days, then, instead of taking time to hear about Krsna, going to nightclubs, movies, restaurants, or other such places in the evening. In these places, they concentrate on women, politics, and sex. Perhaps they drink wine and eat meat. They do not use their time to hear about Krsna. Such persons are *mudha-dhi,* ignorant about life’s actual necessities. Such people are often responsible enough to fulfill their material obligations, but have no knowledge of their spiritual obligations. Thus they remain confused and don’t really understand the goal of their own work.

Therefore, it is essential that *grhasthas* hear about Krsna every day. Narada Muni uses the word *abhiksnam* (always) and Prabhupada expands the meaning of *abhiksnam* to “hearing again and again. ” There is no question of stopping our hearing. Even if we have read Prabhupada’s books many times, we should continue to read them, continue to hear the same topics over and over, because the words spoken by Krsna and His devotees are nectar. Narada Muni asserts that the result of constantly hearing the Lord’s pastimes is attachment and spiritual affection. At the same time, our material affections diminish. If we are to gradually become detached from matter, the prescribed formula to achieve that goal is constant hearing and chanting.

Narada Muni doesn’t call Yudhisthira Maharaja an attached householder and then order him to abandon everything he has. Rather, he tells Yudhisthira Maharaja to attain detachment gradually. We become gradually detached by taking regular doses of the medicine prescribed to rescue us from our material illness. Pariksit Maharaja calls the Lord’s pastimes the right medicine to counteract the effects of repeated birth and death. If we regularly hear the Lord’s pastimes in the association of the Lord’s devotees, we will begin to understand that this material world is merely a dream; the

Supreme Lord is the transcendental reality, and all of His pastimes are true.

Reality, as Krsna describes it in the *Bhagavad-gita,* means “that which does not end, ” or “that which has duration. ” Persons who are aware of the Absolute Truth understand the difference between what exists and what does not. That which exists is eternal, and that which does not is temporary. The Lord’s pastimes are eternal, and material activities are not. They are part of the material dream.

We must awaken by hearing Krsna’s pastimes, because even the devotees’ dreams within Krsna’s pastimes are reality, whereas our apparent waking life in the material world is not. That the pastimes are eternal and real is their wonderful nature. Once, Sacimata told Lord Caitanya confidentially that she had had a dream. At this time, Lord Caitanya was still *a grhastha,* married to Laksmipriya, and both of them were living with His mother. Mother Saci told Lord Caitanya that in her dream, she had seen both Lord Caitanya and Lord Nityananda coming out of the deity room carrying the deities of Krsna and Balarama. In her dream, the four Lords began to fight one another. Krsna and Balarama were screaming at Lord Caitanya and Nityananda, “Get out of here, You fakes! Don’t You know that this is *Our* house? Don’t You know that all the offerings of butter, yogurt, and milk belong to Us? ”

Then Nityananda spoke up and reprimanded Krsna and Balarama. “Look, You aren’t cowherd boys anymore. You can’t get away with stealing milk, butter, and yogurt. You were cowherd boys in the past, but now You are *brahmanas. Brahmanas* are supposed to be renounced. You should renounce all this yogurt, butter, and milk. If You don’t renounce them, certainly You’ll be accused of being thieves. No one likes a thief. ”

Sacimata continued, “In my dream, Krsna and Balarama yelled, ‘Look, the two of You are cheaters and imposters. If We simply take Our ropes, tie You up, and throw You out of the house, no one will say a word! ’ Balarama agreed. ‘Yes, if Krsna tells Me to tie You up, I'll do it! ’”

Sacimata heard Nityananda say in her dream, “Who cares for Your Krsna? Krsna may be Y*our* master, but My master is Caitanya! My master is the Lord of the entire world! I don’t care what Your Krsna says! ”

The fight continued, and every time Lord Nityananda tried to place food in His mouth, Krsna would grab it from Him and put it in His own mouth. Finally, at the end of the dream, Nityananda went to Sacimata and said, “Sacimata, I am hungry. Will you please feed Me? ” That’s when Saci awoke. She asked her son to explain the meaning of her dream.

Lord Caitanya told Sacimata that He had complete faith in her dreams. He assured her that her dream was real and not illusion. He said that the Supreme Personality of Godhead had appeared in the form of the Deities in her home. He said that prior to hearing this dream, He had not understood this. Lord Caitanya said that every time He offered fruits to the Deities and then returned to the altar after some time, He saw that half of the fruits were gone.
