He was actually glorifying the devotees. Just like He provoked Kholaveca Sridhara. He tried to provoke him; He would argue with him. He behaved in a very arrogant way, but He glorified the devotee. Although He tried to agitate the mind of the devotee, Kholaveca Sridhara would always display the quality of peacefulness. The same thing happened when the Lord was engaged in debate with devotees. He tried to agitate them, but their minds were just so attracted by Him that they could not get angry with Him. They would simply speak about Him. ”

Nimai’s students were also absorbed in thought of Him. He was their leader and the best debater in Navadvipa. If the students heard that an argument was to take place, they would put forward Nimai to debate. When Kesava Kasmiri came to Navadvipa, he had already heard of Nimai Pandita’s fame. Nimai’s students were eager to see the Lord defeat the *dig-vijaya* because they were so proud of their teacher.

Of course, when the Lord engaged in argument and debate, it was not *prajalpa.* Similarly, when the devotees discuss the Lord’s argument and debate, that is not *prajalpa* either but rather favorable *krsna-katha.* The Lord behaved like a person absorbed in materialistic talk, but from the incident with Mukunda we know that His wonderful plan was to reveal Himself as the best Vaisnava.

## Favorable and unfavorable prajalpa (2)

**Bhaktyaloka Odessa, July 1999**

We have discussed *prajalpa* as meaning “people speaking together. ” Therefore, there is favorable *prajalpa* and unfavorable *prajalpa.* Speaking about Nimai Pandita engaged in argument is favorable *prajalpa.* It’s interesting to know that the Vaisnavas used to speak about Nimai, even before they knew He was God. The *gopis* too spoke about Krsna in their various groups, and their speaking is called *prajalpa.* When Srimati Radharani spoke to the bumblebee, she spoke various types of *jalpa*—*citra-jalpa, prajalpa, sujalpa,* and *ajalpa*—each of which expressed a different mood in Her relationship with Krsna. No one in Goloka speaks *prajalpa* in the negative sense. Wouldn’t it be nice if devotees in our temples could gather in small groups according to their various moods and discuss Krsna’s pastimes? That would immediately eradicate the tendency to speak negative *prajalpa.*

We have already discussed gossip, argument and debate. In *Bhaktyaloka,* Bhaktivinoda Thakura explains that all the arguments of Nyaya and Vaisesika schools are godless, because they are built on the idea that the cause of creation is material. He writes, “By arguing about directions, places, mistakes, and illusions, the heart becomes tough. The mind is naturally peaceful in Krsna consciousness. When one has situated within his intelligence Krsna consciousness in *buddhi-yoga,* then he has the power to properly discriminate. ”

To have spiritual intelligence means to be able to discriminate between matter, spirit, and the controller of both. When the intelligence is fixed in Krsna consciousness, we possess the ability to discriminate, but when we argue about direction, place, mistakes, and illusions, we toughen our hearts. In other words, when we argue about subjects with no connection to Krsna, we can never reach a truthful conclusion. The arguments are not based on *siddhanta* and do not seek such truth. In the *Bhagavatam’s* sixth canto, it is stated, “Let me offer my respectful obeisances unto the all-pervading Supreme Personality of Godhead, who possesses unlimited transcendental qualities. Acting from within the cores of the hearts of all philosophers, who propagate various views, He causes them to forget their own souls while sometimes agreeing and sometimes disagreeing among themselves. Thus He creates within this material world a situation in which they are unable to come to a conclusion. I offer my obeisances unto Him. ” (SB [6.4.31](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-6/4.31))

This verse describes how the Lord inspires material arguments within the hearts of those who wish to engage in them. Therefore, such persons continue to argue about material forms, illusions, and mistakes and never reach any conclusion. Since these topics cannot be resolved, people who argue them become progressively disappointed and their minds remain disturbed, their hearts hard.

It is not possible for such persons to solve their arguments, because there are no solutions in the material world. The *only* solution to material life is Krsna consciousness. Those who take to Krsna consciousness find their tough hearts softening. That is because they have accepted the conclusion presented by the *mahajanas.* The Krsna conscious conclusion allows a person to become happy and self-satisfied in his personal connection with Krsna. When we feel our connection with Krsna, we become peaceful. “One who is not connected with the Supreme \[in Krsna consciousness\] can have neither transcendental intelligence nor a steady mind, without which there is no possibility of peace. And how can there be any happiness without peace? ” (Bg. [2.66](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/2.66))

Therefore, a devotee wants to accept whatever is favorable for their Krsna consciousness and reject those things that are not.

Arguing about mundane topics is not favorable. Bhaktivinoda Thakura explains that the process of argument itself is not wrong. It is acceptable even for devotees to try to reach the *siddhanta* by presenting various sastric arguments. As students, we should be engaged in discussions that reach the proper conclusion. We also should not reject the proper conclusion, thinking it controversial. Arguments leading to *siddhanta* actually strengthen the mind and help it become attached to Krsna. Bhaktivinoda Thakura writes, “By accepting the Vedic *dasa-mula* instructions and arguing accordingly, one’s mind does not become wicked. ”

How do we know the difference between good and bad? When our deliberations are based on the *Vedas,* we are no longer speaking negative *prajalpa.* Therefore, in *Caitanya-caritamrta* Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu instructs, “Study *Srimad-Bhagavatam* very scrutinizingly. Discussions to establish the knowledge of one’s relationship with the Lord are not *prajalpa.* Those who conquer the assembly by useless argument do not reach any conclusion. It is certainly one’s duty to give up such logicians’ association. ”

What makes *prajalpa* positive or negative is the motivation behind it. When argument is born of pride and the desire to win fame, this is unfavorable. When the argument is based upon a desire to establish *siddhanta,* that is positive.

Of course, devotees do not want to engage in aggressive argumentation; it should not be offensive. Parties on both sides of the argument should want to find the *siddhanta.* Bhaktivinoda Thakura gives the example of the *dasa-mula*—the ten roots or theistic principles of the *Gaudiya Vaisnava* philosophy. The first *mula* states that the Vedic literature is the source of knowledge about the Absolute Truth. The second *mula* states that Hari, Krsna, is the Supreme Lord. The third, that Krsna is always invested with infinite power; the fourth, that Krsna is the ocean of *rasa;* the fifth, that the soul is Krsna’s separated part; the sixth, that certain souls are engaged in Krsna’s illusory energy; the seventh, that certain souls are released from Krsna’s illusory energy; the eighth, that all spiritual and material phenomena are simultaneously one and different from Krsna; the ninth, that pure devotional service is the only means to attain the final objective of life; and the tenth, that pure love of God is life’s final objective. These ten principles comprise *sambandha, abhidheya,* and *prayojana*—the first to the eighth *mulas* teach *sambandha*, the ninth, *abhidheya,* and the last, *prayojana. Sambandha, abhidheya,* and *prayojana* explain what our relationship with God is, how to act according to that relationship, and the relationship’s perfection, culminating in pure *bhakti.*

Pure devotional service is the only means to attain life’s final objective. Bhaktivinoda Thakura states that we discuss these things so we can understand these foundational principles. Any discussion whose conclusion falls short of the conclusions of *dasa-mula* is not *siddhanta.* Therefore, to reach the proper *siddhanta,* devotees are encouraged to engage in discussion. Such discussion is not *prajalpa* in the negative sense.

Sarvabhauma Bhattacarya makes a statement in the *Caitanyacaritamrta* that distinguishes the argumentation of the logicians from the discussion of *krsna-katha:* “In the association of the jackals known as logicians, I simply continued to bark a resounding ‘bheu bheu. ’ Now, from the same mouth I am chanting the holy names ‘Krsna’ and ‘Hari. ’” (Cc. *Madhya* [12.183](https://vedarama.com/en/books/cc-madhya/12.183)) Sarvabhauma Bhattacarya had been a *smarta* logician, but once he became converted by Lord Caitanya, he renounced his *prajalpa* arguments and began to practice pure *bhakti.* His heart had softened when he had heard the words, *“mukti-pade sa daya-bhak. ”* Previously, Sarvabhauma had been attached to the concept of liberation, but after his conversion, he could barely hear the word *mukti.* Instead, he wanted to change the phrase to *“bhakti-pade. ”* Of course, Lord Caitanya told him he could not change the *sastra,* but Lord Caitanya was pleased to see the transformation in him. Now Sarvabhauma could think only of *bhakti,* even though he had once argued with the logicians in his area—not to establish *Vaisnava siddhanta,* but rather as *prajalpa.* “The atheists, headed by Mayavadi philosophers, do not care for liberation or Krsna’s mercy. They simply continue to put forward false arguments and counter-theories of atheistic philosophy, not considering or engaging in spiritual matters. ”

Useless arguments arise from envy and pride. Aversion or attachments to sense gratification are caused either by foolishness or self-pride. Quarrelsome people become naturally intoxicated by useless argument. While discussing topics of the Lord and His servants, practicing devotees should avoid useless arguments. We should always be careful not to create useless arguments. Bhaktivinoda Thakura concludes, “It arises from envy, pride, attachment to sense gratification, and foolishness. ” If we find ourselves having to come up with different arguments just so that we will not look smaller than others, we should know that we are thinking contrary to the Vaisnava mentality. Vaisnavas always think themselves to be servants of other Vaisnavas. They do not argue out of pride but only for the reasons Bhaktivinoda Thakura has stated. Vaisnavas argue with humility; humility is their strength.

When Lord Caitanya defeated Prakasananda Sarasvati, it was His humility that first overwhelmed Prakasananda Sarasvati and his Mayavadi sannyasi followers. Lord Caitanya chose to sit in the place where they washed their feet, then manifested an effulgence that attracted their minds. He thought of Himself and presented Himself as insignificant, and that humility gave Him power. Of course, He was the Supreme Lord, but it was through humility that He chose to attract the other *sannyasis.* When a devotee is humble, he is attractive. Lord Caitanya advises:

*trnad api sunicena taror api sahisnuna*  
*amanina manadena kirtaniyah sada harih*

**In a humble state of mind, we should be ready to offer all respects to others, even if those others have come to a wrong conclusion by our estimation. We must respect everyone, especially if they are devotees.**

In his commentary on the eleventh canto, Srila Jiva Gosvami describes that one of the main symptoms of a *kanistha-adhikari* is that he has difficulty respecting others in his preaching because he can’t even respect devotees. “According to Srila Jiva Gosvami, since a *kanistha-adhikari* who cannot properly respect other devotees will certainly fail to offer respects to ordinary living entities who are not even devotees, a *kanistha-adhikari* is useless for practical preaching work unless he comes to a higher platform of understanding. ” If we can’t respect devotees, how will we respect nondevotees? If we cannot respect nondevotees, what use is our preaching? To respect others, we have to see Krsna present in their hearts. Without that vision, how can we preach? Persons who lack such vision find it easy to argue. Instead of arguing with others, they should submit themselves to their seniors and become defeated by them so that they can advance in spiritual life.

## Gossip—if speech is not regulated, then enmity arises from talking about others

**Bhaktyaloka Odessa, July 1999**

Gossip means talking without reason about others. This is extremely adverse to the practice of devotional service. Many people talk about others in order to establish their own reputations. Being naturally envious, they become accustomed to discuss others’ characters. Those who are busy with such topics can never become fixed on Krsna’s lotus feet any more than those who are accustomed to arguing about mundane topics can become connected to Krsna consciousness. Bhaktivinoda Thakura states that persons who are accustomed to speaking about others without reason can never fix their minds on Krsna.

Of course, I am sure we have all noticed the two significant words in Bhaktivinoda Thakura’s instruction: without reason. Then what reasons are there to discuss others? Obviously, if we try hard enough, any of us can justify talking about others, but what are the reasons Bhaktivinoda Thakura lists as justifications?

Bhaktivinoda Thakura points out that most discussions of others’ characters are meant to establish our own reputation. We tend to cast others in a diminished light, while presenting ourselves as better. Sometimes we tell people we know confidences about others. We think that if we repeat these confidences, we will seem intimate both with them and with the persons about whom we are speaking. These confidences are not spiritual confidences but usually have to do with problems another person is having. When we repeat these confidences, we prove that we do not care about that person’s welfare. Do we ask ourselves, “What am I accomplishing by revealing information about this person? I am presenting myself as all-knowing and at the same time, diminishing him or her. ” This is not a good reason to speak about others.

We are often careless in how we speak about others, and we rarely think about the consequences of our words. We are so absorbed in our own reputations that we don’t think about how others will feel. This is why elsewhere in *Bhaktyaloka,* Srila Bhaktivinoda Thakura makes a similar point: “With the desire to speak more, a person becomes talkative. If speech is not regulated, then enmity arises from talking about others. ” We have the urge to speak and so we speak. Often we speak without thinking. We have no buffer between mind and tongue, so whatever comes to mind comes out of our mouths.

Therefore, we must learn to be careful what we say about others. Whenever we begin to talk among ourselves, we will soon find ourselves talking about others. Better we discuss Krsna. When we talk about Krsna—what He did or what He said—we find that everything is perfect. His pure devotees and their pastimes with Him are all perfect and therefore safe to discuss. Whatever statements we make about these pastimes are authorized as long as we speak on the basis of scripture. Imagine talking about what Sarvabhauma Bhattacarya said to Lord Caitanya. Someone may ask, “Where did you get this information about Sarvabhauma Bhattacarya? ” and we can then go on to discuss what we read in *Caitanya-caritamrta.*

We all have the tendency to speak about others. It is only safe to speak about Krsna and His confidential devotees. As soon as we talk about others, we tend to descend into gossip and other unfavorable modes of speaking. If we catch ourselves doing that, we should look at ourselves and examine our motives. Is it pride that prompts us? Are we trying to establish our own qualities by putting down others? Are we envious? Malicious? And what will the result be for us? Bhaktivinoda Thakura writes, “Persons who are always engrossed in such topics cannot absorb their minds in Krsna. ”

In the *Srimad-Bhagavatam,* Narada Muni states that a devotee has no material possessions and is satisfied by simply possessing the Lord’s service. “Those who are proud of possessing such things often deride devotees of the Lord. Even if such persons offer the Lord worship, the Lord will never accept their worship. ” Krsna will not accept the worship of a person who derides His devotees.

We almost always deride others out of pride. Perhaps we are proud of our wealth, intelligence, or physical beauty. Whatever it is, when we deride others, we are saying that we think we have more material qualifications than the person we are criticizing. We may even be correct. But what we have failed to see is the other person’s devotion or potential for devotion. We are blind to what matters most. We can’t see the devotion of others because we are engrossed in our own material qualifications. We are seeing only matter; we are devoid of spiritual vision. If we can see only materially and we offend Krsna’s devotees, Krsna will not accept our worship.

If Krsna doesn’t accept our worship, we have no connection with Krsna. We must be careful. When Sacimata committed even a slight offense to Advaita Acarya, Lord Caitanya arranged for her to take the dust of Advaita Acarya’s feet. Vrndavana dasa Thakura states, “Even if a person has attained Krsna’s mercy but offends a Vaisnava, he will be stopped from attaining ecstatic love for Krsna. ” In Sacimata’s case, Srivasa Pandita had asked Lord Caitanya to give Sacimata ecstatic love of God. Lord Caitanya refused. He explained that although Sacimata was His mother, she had offended a Vaisnava. Even she could not overcome this injunction.

When Srivasa heard Lord Caitanya’s explanation, he went and explained everything to Advaita Acarya. He told Advaita that He should allow Sacimata to take the dust of His lotus feet so that she could attain ecstatic love for the Lord. When Advaita Acarya heard this, He thought, “How is this possible? How can Sacimata take dust from My feet? She is the Supreme Lord’s mother, nondifferent from Yasoda or Devaki. By simply reciting her name the world becomes auspicious. I am supposed to become her servant. She didn’t offend Me. ” As He began to speak more of Sacimata’s glories, He felt such ecstasy that He fell to the ground in bliss and lost consciousness. Sacimata took that opportunity to take dust from His feet. Then she too began to experience such ecstasy that she also fell to the ground. Such is Advaita Acarya’s power: He had the power to defeat Sacimata. And such was the power of Sacimata: she was able to defeat Advaita Acarya.

Vrndavana dasa Thakura explains further that one who calls one Vaisnava big and another small may be peaceful for now, but how long will he remain so? Under the pretext of rebuking His mother, Lord Caitanya, the Supreme Teacher, taught everyone to avoid offending Vaisnavas. Anyone who jumps over Lord Caitanya’s order and foolishly offends any Vaisnava will be punished for the offense.

Therefore, Vrndavana dasa Thakura suggests that we be careful and not take sides or argue with other Vaisnavas. In the name of glorifying a big Vaisnava, we may find ourselves offending a small Vaisnava. This is dangerous for our spiritual lives.

I have been thinking about this concept and discussing it with Radhanatha Maharaja. We both agreed that in the name of glorifying one Vaisnava, we should be careful not to offend another. Lord Caitanya will not tolerate it. If we receive Krsna’s mercy but offend His devotees, He will not give us ecstatic love of God.

There are many ways to offend others. Therefore, we must be careful in how we speak. Part of being careful is examining the motives behind the things we say. Certainly pride and envy can never stand as reasons for *prajalpa.* But there are two categories of renunciation of *prajalpa.* “Those who have become renunciates have no business whatsoever engaging in sense enjoyment, and therefore, they can renounce speaking about others in all respects. Householders, on the other hand, are engaged in earning money and in maintaining and protecting their family members. Therefore, they cannot completely give up talking about others. In order to avoid *aparadha,* it would be better for them to live in a Krsna conscious family where all their activities are related to Krsna. Then even this unavoidable talk about others will become a sinless part of devotional service. ” (Sri *Bhaktyaloka,* Bhaktivinoda Thakura)

Whether householder or renunciate, we should not speak about others in a way that is detrimental to them. Rather, we should speak about others only as much as is necessary. Neither should we speak about them without good reason. For example, to earn money householders have to speak about others. If he must work with persons disinterested in spiritual life, he should not associate with them. Association is defined as exchanging affection and confidences. Sometimes our householder devotees say that their consciousness is being affected by the association of their nondevotee work partners, but they should not be associating with them. If we find ourselves identifying with them or feeling affection for them, then our Krsna consciousness is at risk. When we feel affection toward materialistic persons, it means we also feel affection toward their materialistic qualities. It is those qualities with which we don’t want to become enamored. In his work, a householder must be with other people, but he must also always keep his spiritual objective in mind as he performs his prescribed duties.

Neither should the householder feel that he should not be working just so he can avoid any bad association. A householder’s duty, according to Narada Muni in the seventh canto of the *Bhagavatam,* is to earn an honest livelihood to support his family and to offer the fruits of his labor to Krsna. Narada Muni explains this in reply to Yudhisthira Maharaja’s question: “How does one who is a *grhastha* and ignorant of the goal of life, achieve the liberation that is prescribed in the *Vedas? ”* A householder must make a living, but his goal should be to maintain his family in such a way that they all have time to hear and chant about Krsna.

When we remember the objective, everything settles into its proper perspective. Bhaktivinoda Thakura also discusses this point in his *Bhaktyaloka* under “Enthusiasm. ” Often devotees don’t feel enthusiastic in their work, because they are afraid to become entangled in matter. Still, they have to work out of duty. Bhaktivinoda Thakura explains that a person can be enthusiastic to work if he maintains his body just so he can hear and chant about Krsna. We cannot cultivate devotional service without a material body. If we are completely Krsna conscious and fully engaged in devotional service, then we do not need the body, because we are not atthe stage of cultivating *bhakti.* For those of us who are cultivating it, we must hear about Krsna as often as possible. It is important, then, to maintain the body to enable us to hear. This is also the primary objective in maintaining our family members.

The *Bhagavatam* states that life’s desire should never be directed toward sense gratification. We should desire to live only because the human form enables us to inquire about the Absolute Truth. This should be the goal of all our work. We should not work only for the sake of enjoying what the body can provide. What is the value of maintaining the body if we don’t become Krsna conscious in this life? But if we work to maintain our bodies and the bodies of our family members simply so we can all hear about Krsna, then the work has meaning. If we must talk about others, if our material activities are related to Krsna, then such unavoidable talk will remain sinless and a part of our devotional practices.

Bhaktivinoda Thakura mentions a couple of other reasons why we may speak about others. “When the guru enlightens his disciple on a topic, unless he occasionally talks about others, his instructions may not be clear. When previous *mahajanas* have spoken about others in this way, there was merit in such talk, not fault. Sukadeva Gosvami states in the *Srimad-Bhagavatam,* ‘The lifetime of such an envious householder is passed at night either in sleeping or sex indulgence and in the daytime either making money or maintaining family members. Persons devoid of *atma-tattva* do not inquire into the problems of life, being too attached to fallible soldiers like the body, children and wife. Although sufficiently experienced, they still do not see their inevitable destruction. ’ ” Here Sukadeva Gosvami is speaking about envious householders and what they do. Someone may ask, “Why is Sukadeva Gosvami speaking in that way? ” But Bhaktivinoda Thakura says that the spiritual master must sometimes speak about others in order to illustrate his points. We often need an example to justify what we are doing. It’s our nature to seek such examples. If we don’t want to follow the higher standard, then the tendency is to look for someone popular who doesn’t follow that standard and to justify our own failings. We look for examples to justify what *we* like to do. The spiritual master will therefore use examples in order to make his points clear and to point out which examples we should not follow. Although Sukadeva Gosvami talks about the materialists in order to instruct his disciple, he is not considered a *prajalpi.* His talk was beneficial.

It is also true that Sukadeva Gosvami did not spend his entire time speaking gossip. Rather, he taught Krsna consciousness to Maharaja Pariksit, and then used an example from the lives of envious householders. Sukadeva Gosvami was not talking idly about householders. When I think of the word “idle, ” I think of a car idling, sitting in one place with the engine running. In English, we sometimes call someone a “motor-mouth” if they speak too much about nothing. When we speak gossip, we let our engine run, but we don’t go anywhere.

Bhaktivinoda Thakura describes Sukadeva Gosvami’s use of gossip to explain that we should not speak about others without reason. One of the reasons is to conclude a point while giving an instruction. Obviously, the person speaking must be in a position to be able to give instruction. Giving instructions to others is not everyone’s privilege. This is another important point to note. Ultimately, we must learn to think before we speak, and we should focus our minds on Krsna.

Bhaktivinoda Thakura also gives another example in *Bhaktyaloka,* this time of Lord Caitanya. Bhaktivinoda Thakura tells the story of Lord Caitanya and Chota Haridasa. This story is an example of Lord Caitanya speaking about someone in order to instruct His disciples. The Lord’s words were strong: “The Lord replied, ‘I cannot tolerate seeing the face of a person who has accepted the renounced order of life but who still talks intimately with a woman... There are many persons with little in their possession who accept the renounced order of life like monkeys. They go here and there engaging in sense gratification and speaking intimately with women’. ” (Cc. *Antya* [2.117](https://vedarama.com/en/books/cc-antya/2.117), [120](https://vedarama.com/en/books/cc-antya/2.120))

Bhaktivinoda Thakura states, “Unless one speaks in this way while instructing and while concluding a subject, there is no benefit for oneself or others. ” Therefore, the previous *acaryas* both taught by example and gave examples of others so that we could benefit from following the *acaryas’* instructions.

Lord Caitanya certainly taught His disciples by using the example of Chota Haridasa. The Lord was, of course, in a position of being able to give instruction, and even though Chota Haridasa’s offense was not actually severe, Lord Caitanya used him to teach His point. As Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura explains in his commentary, because Lord Caitanya knew that in the future persons would accept the renounced order (especially the *sahajiya babajis)* so that they could more easily associate intimately with women, He spoke strongly about Chota Haridasa.

But what had Chota Haridasa done? Bhagavan Acarya invited Lord Caitanya to his home for lunch. He wanted to prepare wonderful, opulent food. He wanted to use first-class rice, so he told Chota Haridasa that Madhavi Devi, one of Lord Caitanya’s most intimate associates, had first-class rice. He then asked Chota Haridasa to please beg some from her. Madhavi Devi was an exalted Vaisnavi and an elderly woman. Haridasa begged the rice from her and brought it back for Lord Caitanya’s meal.

Later that day, Lord Caitanya came for lunch and Bhagavan Acarya served Him everything he had cooked. The first preparation the Lord tasted was the wonderful rice. He said, “This rice is so tasty. Where did you get it? ”

Bhagavan Acarya told the Lord that he had sent Chota Haridasa to beg it from Madhavi Devi. Lord Caitanya didn’t comment but simply continued to eat His meal.

When He had finished, He said to Govinda dasa, “From this day on, Chota Haridasa should not come and see Me. He can never associate with Me again. ”

Govinda dasa was bewildered by the Lord’s words and he repeated them to several of the Vaisnavas. The Vaisnavas began to talk among themselves. Why had the Lord taken such a strong position? When Chota Haridasa heard the Lord’s position, he began to fast.

Three days later, Svarupa Damodara went to ask Lord Caitanya what offense Chota Haridasa had committed. “You are the independent Supreme Personality of Godhead. You can do whatever You wish. But at least allow Your servant to someday return to Your association. ”

When Svarupa Damodara said this, Lord Caitanya made His heavy statement: “I cannot tolerate seeing the face of a person who has accepted the renounced order of life and still talks intimately with a woman. ” Svarupa Damodara related this information back to the *Vaisnavas.* Then the various *Vaisnavas* began to speak on Chota Haridasa’s behalf, but whenever they tried to speak to Lord Caitanya, He said, “I do not want to hear this subject. If you say one more thing, I will leave. I will not come back. ” When the devotees heard this, they covered their ears. The Lord’s words were like a knife in their hearts. The devotees also knew that Lord Caitanya meant it; He had walked away before.

In the meantime, Chota Haridasa did not know how he could remain alive without the Lord’s association. The devotees tried to convince him that the Lord would be merciful and that he should simply wait for some time. “There is a reason why He is angry with you right now, but if you wait, His anger will pass and He will again show you His mercy. ” Chota Haridasa felt encouraged by their kind words and decided to break his fast. Then, whenever Lord Caitanya went to the Jagannatha temple, Chota Haridasa would also go, but would stay at a distance, observing the Lord’s movements.

One year passed in this way, but Caitanya Mahaprabhu did not relent. Again Chota Haridasa became discouraged. Then one night, Chota Haridasa decided to give up his body. He could not find any purpose to his life without the Lord’s association. He entered the Triveni at Prayag and drowned himself.

Shortly after that, Chota Haridasa appeared in a spiritual body that resembled a Gandharva. When Chota Haridasa had been the Lord’s associate, he had always sung for the Lord. Now in his spiritual body, Chota Haridasa again sang for the Lord’s pleasure. No one else but Lord Caitanya could hear his singing.

One day, the Lord asked the devotees, “Where is Chota Haridasa? Tell him he can return to see Me. ” Of course, Lord Caitanya is omniscient; He already knew where Haridasa had gone. But this was His pastime and the devotees related the sad news. When Lord Caitanya heard about Chota Haridasa’s death, He smiled a little. The devotees could not understand the meaning of His smile. Then when they went to the sea to bathe, they heard in the distance something that sounded like Chota Haridasa singing. They began to discuss the phenomenon among themselves. This too was not *prajalpa;* the devotees were trying to understand a truth. They had all assumed that because Chota Haridasa had committed suicide, he had been forced to take the body of a ghost. Therefore, they assumed that what they were hearing was a ghost singing. When Svarupa Damodara heard their statements, he said, “No, that is not possible. You will soon see that Haridasa did not obtain the body of a ghost. Haridasa was the Lord’s servant, and he gave up his body in an auspicious place. ”

Later the devotees understood that Chota Haridasa had obtained his spiritual body, and they could then understand why the Lord had smiled upon hearing of his death. There are various commentaries on this pastime because it is so instructive, and aside from the lessons that Caitanya Mahaprabhu was trying to teach about renunciation, we can also understand it in relation to gossip. Lord Caitanya spoke about Chota Haridasa while instructing His followers and concluding the subject. Bhaktivinoda Thakura explains, “If one discusses in this way the improper behavior which is current in religious sects or among the general public, then such topics are not adverse to devotional service. ” In this way, we can learn about proper and improper behavior. Our statements should also reach a conclusion. People should not walk away from such instruction without understanding the point. They should also not walk away feeling compelled to gossip more about the persons in the example. Rather, they should understand the philosophical point being made through the example.

Bhaktivinoda Thakura gives clear, concise instructions on how to discipline the mind so that when we congregate, we will not run the engine while going nowhere. By the way, when Lord Caitanya instructed His devotees about Chota Haridasa’s behavior, He did not even mention his name. He spoke only about persons in the renounced order who commit a particular offense. But Bhaktivinoda Thakura states that even if the person’s name is mentioned for the purpose to instruct others, there is no fault, and he gives the example of Maitreya Muni speaking about Maharaja Vena in the Fourth Canto. Maitreya Muni said plainly, “Thus the king, who became unintelligent due to his sinful life and deviation from the path became bereft of all good fortune. ” Bhaktivinoda Thakura writes, “Sri Maitreya Rsi needed to speak about him in this way in order to instruct his audience. It was not *prajalpa.* Practicing devotees normally discuss ancient history in the association of other devotees. Occasionally they talk about nondevotees. Such talks are always auspicious and favorable to devotional service, whereas those who speak about others while influenced by devotional impediments like envy, hatred, pride, or the desire for distinction are offenders at the feet of Bhakti-devi. ”

In practical terms, devotees sometimes have to struggle with the instruction not to speak *prajalpa.* For example, we are not always in control of how particular conversations go. Sometimes we may begin by speaking *krsna-katha,* but others introduce gossip into the conversation.

If we are in a position to instruct the devotees speaking *prajalpa*— that is, if they are our juniors—then we should explain to them the dangers of speaking *prajalpa.* If we are not in a position to instruct, however, as we would not be if we were addressing our seniors, then the best thing to do would be submissively ask about the purpose of the discussion. Perhaps what the devotee is speaking about will fit Bhaktivinoda Thakura’s criteria for good reasons to speak *prajalpa.* But we should ask respectfully. It may well be that because the senior devotee does not want us to do something that the person in his example has done, he is using the information to instruct us.

If the senior devotee is actually gossiping, then we can respectively submit that we do not understand the purpose of the conversation. We can also say, “I prefer not to hear gossip. ” Any devotee should respect that. If the devotee is blaspheming someone and we feel that his or her words are completely unnecessary, we may leave the place. But we should be careful not to jump to conclusions and to too easily find fault with our seniors. If we think the senior does not have a proper understanding, then it would be better not to instruct him but to inform a higher authority—someone who will be capable of dealing with the situation.

If we do not have a reason to speak about others, we can be certain that doing so will negatively affect our spiritual lives. But this is not true if we have reason to discuss about others (and we have already explained the reasons). We should also take into consideration Bhaktivinoda Thakura’s distinction between householders and renunciates. Bhaktivinoda Thakura himself organized *sankirtana* among householders in order to spread the holy name. He started the *sankirtana* movement without temples or institutions. Srila Bhaktisiddhanta institutionalized preaching and opened temples to house the renunciates. Nowadays, the temple president has the responsibility to earn, maintain, and protect the temple devotees. His duty is to organize the temple in such a way that all these objectives are being fulfilled. In that sense, he is working in the same way a householder works—cooperatively to maintain his own body and those of his congregational members so that all of us can hear about Krsna. If a temple leader speaks about others with this objective in mind, his discussions are appropriate, because they are not without reason. Still, he should always speak without unnecessary criticism born of envy, pride, hatred, or his own desire for distinction.

## Anyone who blasphemes Vaisnavas will suffer

**Bhaktyaloka Odessa, July 1999**

Another form of *prajalpa* is blaspheming *sadhus.* Bhaktivinoda Thakura writes, “Speech in the form of blaspheming *sadhus* is the source of great inauspiciousness. If one wants to obtain devotion to Lord Hari, then he should make a vow like this, ‘In this life I will never blaspheme *sadhus. ’* ”

Sometimes devotees speak about performing one *vrata* or another, but if we want to advance in spiritual life, we should take this personal vow mentioned by Bhaktivinoda Thakura. Every devotee should learn this principle. The *sastras* contain many examples to show us the danger of blaspheming *sadhus.* If we blaspheme devotees, all our virtues are destroyed. By blaspheming the supremely pure Lord Mahadeva, Prajapati Daksa met with “severe inauspiciousness. ” The *Srimad-Bhagavatam* states: “My dear king, when a great man persecutes great souls all his benedictions of longevity, beauty, fame, religion, blessings and promotion to higher planets will be destroyed. ” (SB [10.4.46](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-10/4.46)) In this verse, Sukadeva Gosvami describes six things we can lose if we criticize great souls: life duration, beauty (Gopala Cakravarti’s nose melted right on his face after offending Haridasa Thakura), fame, blessings (we cannot accept blessings from Vaisnavas because we are criticizing Vaisnavas), religion, and our ability to be promoted to higher planets. The *Skanda Purana* describes six types of hells to which we will be taken if we strike a Vaisnava, criticize a Vaisnava, refuse to greet him, maintain anger toward him, or even if we are not happy to see him. Don’t neglect Vaisnavas. We should always be happy to receive the Vaisnava as a guest.

Worse than not happily greeting Vaisnavas, of course, is criticizing them, hating them, or maintaining any kind of enmity toward them. Bhaktivinoda Thakura cites the story of Daksa offending Lord Siva. In that story, Sati tells Daksa, “It is not wonderful for persons who have accepted the transient material body as the self to engage always in deriding great souls. Such envy on the part of materialistic persons is very good because that is the way they fall down. They are diminished by the dust of the feet of great personalities. ” (SB [4.4.13](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-4/4.13)) Envy is not good, but by envying Vaisnavas, offenders are diminished by the dust of the Vaisnavas’ feet. Prabhupada explains in his purport to this verse:

“Everything depends on the strength of the recipient. For example, due to the scorching sunshine many vegetables and flowers dry up, and many grow luxuriantly. Thus it is the recipient that causes growth and dwindling... the dust of the lotus feet of great personalities offers all good to the recipient, but the same dust can also do harm. Those who are offenders at the lotus feet of a great personality dry up; their godly qualities diminish. A great soul may forgive offenses, but Krsna does not excuse offenses to the dust of that great soul’s feet, just as one can tolerate the scorching sunshine on one’s head but cannot tolerate the scorching sunshine on one’s feet. An offender glides down more and more; therefore he naturally continues to commit offenses at the feet of the great soul. Offenses are generally committed by persons who falsely identify with the impermanent body... Thus they are subject to commit more and more offenses at the lotus feet of great souls. One who has such a concept of life is considered to be in the class of animals like cows and asses. ”

The dust from the feet of Vaisnavas acts according to the recipient. If a person renders service to the feet of great devotees, then the dust of their feet will help him make spiritual advancement.

Those who offend devotees dry up. Daksa suffered the reactions to his offenses to Lord Siva. All offenses to Vaisnavas bring serious reactions, because although the Vaisnavas may overlook our offenses, Krsna will not.

Therefore, how do we avoid blaspheming Vaisnavas? The *Caitanya-bhagavata* tells a pastime which Srila Vrndavana dasa Thakura assures us will cure us of our tendency to blaspheme Vaisnavas: “The person who hears and sings these saintly descriptions will never find blasphemy of a Vaisnava take birth within him. ”

This particular pastime takes place in the village of Kuliya. Lord Caitanya was in hiding, and all the Vaisnavas were looking for Him. This was at the time when He was going from village to village. He would show Himself, then go into hiding again, and all the devotees were simply trying to get a glimpse of Him. Many of them climbed trees or onto the roofs of houses. There were hundreds of thousands of people trying to receive the Lord’s *darsana.*

At that time, a leper approached Lord Caitanya and offered *dandavats.* He was crying with pain, and raised his arms before the Lord and said, “O merciful Lord, You have descended to this world simply to deliver the conditioned souls. Whenever You see someone suffering, You feel great unhappiness. Therefore I have come before You. ”

This leper was correct in his estimation of Lord Caitanya’s mercy. No Vaisnava likes to see another suffering, but Lord Caitanya is the most merciful incarnation of the Lord. He descended to this world specifically to release the conditioned souls from their suffering. Here was a leper burning with pain. Leprosy is an incredibly painful disease, and those who have it say that their skin feels like it’s burning. The leper said, “I am tormented by leprosy. Please tell me how I can be cured. ”

When Lord Caitanya heard the leper’s words, He became angry. “You sinner, you should die! Die! ” The most merciful Lord made such a statement and gave such a benediction. “When the people see you, sin will take birth in them. Even when the saintly persons see you, they will have to suffer. You have blasphemed Vaisnavas. Your actions are evil. You think you are suffering now. Just imagine how much suffering is awaiting you when your life is over. If you cannot bear this suffering now, how will you bear the pain of Kumbhipaka? The word ‘Vaisnava’ purifies the world. Brahma and the demigods always glorify Vaisnavas. Anyone who worships the Vaisnavas obtains Krsna’s lotus feet. Nothing is greater than worshiping Vaisnavas. ”

Lord Caitanya then explained that the *Vaisnavas* are so special because they are dear to the Lord. We should know that this is the essential point to remember when we are dealing with *Vaisnavas: Vaisnavas* are not ordinary persons because they are dear to Krsna. *All Vaisnavas* are dear to Krsna.

In *Caitanya-bhagavata,* the Lord states that all living entities are His servants. Anyone who criticizes any living entity unnecessarily will have to suffer. He orders everyone to give up criticizing others. What to speak of offending others, we should never criticize a Vaisnava, who is most dear to the Lord. Lord Caitanya states, “O Uddhava! Neither Brahma, nor Sankara, nor Sankarsana, nor Laksmi, nor even My own self is as dear to Me as you. ” (Cc. *Adi* [6.102](https://vedarama.com/en/books/cc-adi/6.102))

From *Caitanya-bhagavata:* “Anyone who blasphemes Vaisnavas will suffer. When a Vaisnava dances, the earth is made fortunate and glorious. By a Vaisnava’s glance all the sins in the four directions are nullified. ” About Srivasa Pandita, Lord Caitanya says, “Srivasa Pandita is a great Vaisnava. You blasphemed his character. It is said this flame of leprosy you are experiencing now is only the beginning of your suffering. Yamaraja has much more suffering in store for you. You are not worthy even to be seen by Me. ”

When the leper heard Lord Caitanya’s words, he took a blade of grass between his teeth and said, “I don’t know anything. I hate my own self. I am so crazy that I was blaspheming a Vaisnava. Therefore it is right that I am punished. Still, You are the Supreme Controller. Please, think of my health. A saintly person’s nature is to rescue the sufferer. He will be merciful even to a blasphemer. Therefore I take shelter of You. If You reject me, who will deliver me? ”

We can learn from the leper’s prayer. He recognizes his mistake and accepts that he deserves punishment, but still he asks the Lord to help him rectify himself. In other words, if even the Lord will not forgive us for offending a Vaisnava, what hope do we have? That means we are forever condemned. Is it possible that the Lord would be so merciless?

Some religions state that if we commit certain sins, we will go to hell forever. At some of our Ratha-yatra festivals in the West, we have seen Christians carrying posters that say, “Turn or Burn. ” Either we turn from our offense of “rejecting” Christ, or we will forever burn in hell. But the Lord is actually merciful. This leper was face to face with the Lord. He had no other way of gaining forgiveness.

We must come to understand the Lord’s heart. The Lord will never forgive, but He invests all His forgiveness potency in the *Vaisnavas. Vaisnavas* are very forgiving. If a Vaisnava forgives our offense, Krsna will also forgive us in order to honor His devotee. The Lord does not forgive independent of a Vaisnava. Rather, the Lord will punish. That is, even if a Vaisnava does not recognize an offense that has been committed against him, the Lord will still punish the offender. The Lord retains that right. “O Uddhava, you are more dear to Me than My own self. ” The Lord loves the *Vaisnavas.*

Lord Caitanya was prepared to kill Jagai and Madhai, but when Nityananda begged for their lives, He relented. Because Lord Nityananda forgave their offenses, Lord Caitanya surrendered to Lord Nityananda’s will for them. Such is the power of a Vaisnava.

In the story of Maharaja Ambarisa and Durvasa Muni, Durvasa Muni begged the Lord to protect him from the Sudarsana-cakra.

However, Lord Visnu told him that he would not act independently of His devotee. The devotee controls Krsna. Krsna would not forgive Durvasa but could only suggest that if Durvasa wanted to save himself, he should fall at Ambarisa’s feet.

Lord Caitanya states, “If a person gets a thorn in his foot, what’s the good of massaging his shoulders? Why do you massage up here if the pain is there? ” If we have offended a Vaisnava, why should we rise up to beg Krsna for forgiveness? We must go to the person whom we offended. The Vaisnavas are situated at the Lord’s feet. Why massage the Lord’s shoulders to remove a thorn in His foot?

When Lord Caitanya heard the leper’s plea, He said, “A person who blasphemes a Vaisnava will suffer leprosy. That is written in the scriptures. That is only his present punishment. So much more awaits him by Yamaraja. The blasphemer of a Vaisnava suffers again and again thirteen thousand tortures of Yamaraja. O leper, run to Srivasa Pandita and fall at his feet. Only his mercy can deliver you. Srivasa Pandita must forgive you. Then your suffering will go away. He is intelligent. Go to him. He will forgive you and he will deliver you. ”

It often takes us some time to beg forgiveness for our offenses, and in that time, we suffer needlessly. Sati thinks that it is good that the envious commit offenses so that they can be diminished by the dust from the *Vaisnavas*’ feet. Only when they are diminished and suffering will they beg forgiveness from those whom they have offended. Daksa begged forgiveness only after his head was removed and replaced with the head of a goat. Daksa suffered a lot, but his suffering was good for him. It brought him to the realization that he had been offensive and helped humble his pride.

When we are proud, we don’t think we need to ask forgiveness for our offenses, even when we can recognize that we committed them. Rather, we justify our offenses. Usually we say that we too were offended. But a Vaisnava never thinks that way; he never thinks of himself as elevated. This was the story with Daksa and

Lord Siva. Daksa felt offended by Lord Siva first, because Lord Siva didn’t rise when Daksa entered the sacrificial arena. Daksa didn’t understand Lord Siva’s greatness because he was covered by pride. Later, he insulted Lord Siva. Lord Siva didn’t want anything to do with this exchange of offenses, so he did not attend the sacrifice. But because he is such a great Vaisnava, Daksa had to suffer the consequences for his offending statements. Daksa thought, “If he offended me first, why should I beg his forgiveness? He didn’t show me proper respect. I am, after all, his father-in-law. ” But Lord Siva is the greatest Vaisnava, and Daksa underestimated how dear he was to the Lord.

We can learn from this pastime that pride in our material qualifications blinds us to spiritual reality. Daksa thought of his own high education, wealth, physical beauty, heritage, and austerities with pride. Therefore, he could not appreciate the qualities of a personality as great as Lord Siva. Suffering humbled him. When he was suffering, he thought only of how he could find relief.

Similarly, the leper who offended Srivasa Pandita sought relief from the consequences of his offense only when he was suffering. He was willing at that point to go to Srivasa Pandita to beg forgiveness. Imagine what he felt when he heard Lord Caitanya say, “You can become released from all your suffering if you beg Srivasa Pandita for forgiveness. He will forgive you. ”

The leper immediately went to Srivasa Pandita and fell at his lotus feet. Srivasa Pandita bestowed his mercy upon the leper and the leper was delivered. His offenses were destroyed.

Bhaktivinoda Thakura stresses that we should not take sides when *Vaisnavas* disagree. When we take the side of one Vaisnava, we may inadvertently blaspheme the Vaisnava who has taken the other side. We will then receive a reaction. Vrndavana dasa Thakura writes, “For the sake of being on the side of one Vaisnava and glorifying him, you are blaspheming another Vaisnava. The blasphemer will be punished. ” With one hand such a person serves the Lord and with the other hand he tortures Him. How will any good come of such behavior?

The devotees of Krsna are the Lord’s own body. One who knows this becomes peaceful and serious in spiritual life. A person who sees that they are not different worships Krsna and the Vaisnavas. “He crosses beyond the world of birth and death. A person who hears and sings these sacred descriptions will never find blasphemy of a devotee taking birth within him. ”

If you wish to avoid blaspheming Vaisnavas, simply hear and chant Lord Caitanya’s pastimes. Blasphemy is the last characteristic of *prajalpa* that Bhaktivinoda Thakura discusses.

## Attachments and Material Desires

## How can we see our attachments?

**SB** [**3.26.7**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-3/26.7) **Novosibirsk, November 16, 1996**

We actually have to be advanced before we can detect all the subtle attachments within the heart. Lord Caitanya revealed that to the devotees when they cleaned the Gundica temple. The Lord handed everyone a broom. All the devotees, as well as the Lord, swept the temple, collecting the dust, straw and dirt in a pile outside. Lord Caitanya’s pile was bigger than everyone else’s put together.

After the cleaning had been finished, Lord Caitanya said, “All right, now let’s clean again, this time searching for the fine particles that were not so easy to find the first time we cleaned. ” Then all the devotees again entered the temple and again began to clean. All of them discovered that there were still many grains of sand and tiny pieces of straw that had not been swept up the first time. These fine particles were more difficult to see.

Because this pastime has been compared to the process of *anartha-nivrtti,* we can understand that first we have to remove the large *anarthas* before we will be able to see the smaller ones. These smaller particles are compared to the attachments within the heart. The *Caitanya-caritamrta* lists the attachments: *kuti-nati* (the faultfinding propensity), *jivahimsa* (envy of other living entities), and *puja* (the desire for glory or recognition). All these material desires are subtle. They are the fine particles that must be removed from our hearts before we can invite the Lord to sit there.

Often, we cannot see our *anarthas* ourselves. This is why it is important to associate with other devotees. What we cannot see is often obvious to other devotees; the other devotees act as a mirror to reflect our attachments.

We have probably all experienced periods where we like what we see in ourselves, but often, when our words and behaviors are reflected back to us in the association of devotees, we see ourselves differently. We have probably also all had the experience of seeing our chief attachments—attachments to which we are usually blind— in others.

Ultimately, we have to make ourselves susceptible or open to hearing from others if we wish to clear our hearts. When a sincere godbrother approaches us and says, “Prabhu, I want to tell you something. Please know that I am not trying to offend you. I am your well-wisher. But I perceive a certain type of attachment in you, ” we may think, “Well, who is he to point it out? ” But if we turn ourselves away from that godbrother’s voice, we will not see our own fault and be better able to rectify it. Moreover, we will begin to find fault with our godbrother too. The tendency to find fault in others is quite deep. It means we take our faults and blame them on others. Or, we are so busy looking for faults in others that we do not have time to look for them in ourselves. We become too absorbed in seeing others’ faults.

We have to overcome this obstacle if we wish to deepen our devotional surrender. We have to be willing to hear from others about our faults, and we have to concentrate on rectifying them. We may think, “I will accept this as a fault if Krsna dictates it to me in my heart. ” But Krsna is in *everyone’s* heart. What if Krsna chose, instead of dictating it directly, to have it pointed out by that sincere godbrother? Can’t Krsna do that?

Even if the fault is pointed out by someone we do not respect, a devotee should become expert at taking gold from a filthy place. Even a nondevotee can help us. Krsna can use nondevotees to teach us as easily as He can use devotees.

When Dharma the bull was being beaten by Kali personified, he thought, “Krsna is doing this. ” When Maharaja Pariksit asked him who the culprit was, Dharma told him that the beating had been sanctioned by the Lord. He said that it was therefore not possible to point out the perpetrator, because the Lord’s will is perfect. He said that it was by the Lord’s will that he was suffering. A devotee always thinks like this. Therefore, he is willing to take whatever help Krsna sends—from whatever source. When we have that attitude, Krsna will help us.

This is the science of learning how to see attachments in the heart. For those who are not spiritually advanced, the attachments are difficult to see. Devotional association is invaluable.

## Anarthas can choke the bhakti-lata

**SB** [**3.18.43**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-3/18.43) **September 16, 1996**

Someone may be attracted to the process of devotional service because he thinks he’ll be relieved from suffering. This is the main thing that makes devotional service attractive to such a person. Our experience, however, is different. Most of us find that practicing devotional service does not relieve our suffering, or it produces other kinds of suffering. Sometimes people are disappointed by this and even give up their devotional service in frustration. They are disappointed because they were attached to relief from pain even more than they were attached to being able to taste the nectar at Krsna’s feet. Such persons then go out to search elsewhere for pain relief.

Of course, if even for a moment we receive the taste of Krsna’s lotus feet, we will feel fully satisfied and our sufferings will pale in comparison. We will not be able to forget the taste of devotional service ever again. The trick is, then, to get even a minute taste of real Krsna consciousness.

We get a taste of Krsna consciousness by associating with the Lord. Usually, we get the Lord’s association by associating with His devotees. Even if we fall down after receiving the spiritual taste, we will never forget the intense pleasure we felt and will always want to return to it. We will want to remember that ecstasy again and again.

We will be more open to receiving the taste of devotional service if we clear the heart of *anarthas.* An *anartha* is something unwanted, such as lust, envy, greed, the tendency to criticize others, or desires for worship and fame. We should not cultivate anarthas, but uproot them.

Those who have strong intelligence will understand that they do not want their *anarthas.* At the same time, we don’t want to continuously meditate on them. We can actually strengthen *anarthas* by meditating on them constantly. The scriptures state that *anarthas* can choke the *bhakti-lata.* Therefore we are advised not to maintain material attachments while chanting the Lord’s name. Don’t commit this offense to the holy name.

## How to kill demon-like desires?

**SB 5.18.8 Moscow, May 8, 1996**

*om namo bhagavate narasimhaya*  
*namas tejas-tejase avir-avirbhava*  
*vajra-nakha vajra-damstra karmasayan*  
*randhaya randhaya tamo grasa grasa om svaha*  
*abhayam abhayam atmani bhuyistha om ksraum*

**“I offer my respectful obeisances unto Lord Nrsimhadeva, the source of all power. O my Lord who possesses nails and teeth just like thunderbolts, kindly vanquish our demonlike desires for fruitive activity in this material world. Please appear in our hearts and drive away our ignorance so that by Your mercy we may become fearless in the struggle for existence in this material world. ” (SB** [**5.8.18**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-5/8.18)**)**

PURPORT

“In *Bhagavatam* ([4.22.39](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-4/22.39)), Sanat-kumara speaks the following words to Maharaja Prthu:

*yat-pada-pankaja-palasa-vilasa-bhaktya*  
*karmasayam grathitam udgrathayanti santah*  
*tadvan na rikta-matayo yatayo ’pi ruddha*  
*sroto-ganas tam aranam bhaja vasudevam*

**Devotees always engaged in the service of the toes of the Lord’s lotus feet can very easily become free from the hard-knotted desires for fruitive activities. Because this is very difficult, the nondevotees— the** ***jnanis*** **and** ***yogis*****—cannot stop the waves of sense gratification, although they try to do so. Therefore you are advised to engage in the devotional service of Krsna, the son of Vasudeva. ”**

Every living being in this world has a strong desire to enjoy matter to his fullest satisfaction. Therefore, the conditioned souls accept one body after another, cultivating their fruitive consciousness. But we cannot stop the repetition of birth and death without being desireless. Srila Rupa Gosvami describes pure *bhakti* as follows:

*anyabhilasita-sunyam*  
*jnana-karmady-anavrtam*  
*anukulyena krsnanu*  
*silanam bhaktir uttama*

**“One should render transcendental loving service to the Supreme Lord Krsna favorably and without desire for material profit or gain through fruitive activities or philosophical speculation. That is called pure devotional service. ”**

Srila Prabhupada writes that unless we are completely free from material desire, which is caused by ignorance, we cannot fully engage in the Lord’s service. He suggests that we always offer our prayers to Lord Nrsimhadeva. Lord Nrsimhadeva killed Hiranyakasipu, the personification of material desire. *Hiranya* means gold, and *kasipu* means soft cushion or bed. “Materialistic persons desire to make the body comfortable, and for this they require huge amounts of gold. Thus Hiranyakasipu was the perfect representative of materialistic consciousness. He was also the cause of disturbance to the topmost devotee, Prahlada Maharaja. Any devotee aspiring to free himself of material desire should offer respectful prayers to Lord Nrsimhadeva, as Prahlada Maharaja did in this verse. ”

This is the auspicious day of Lord Nrsimhadeva’s appearance. We often think of Lord Nrsimhadeva’s killing of Hiranyakasipu because of how the Lord appeared in such a fearsome form simply to protect His devotee. We understand that the Lord appeared both to protect Prahlada and to kill Hiranyakasipu, but here we see that Prahlada prays to the Lord to please uproot the fruitive desires in his heart. Hiranyakasipu is the personification of material desire. As Lord Nrsimhadeva killed him, Prahlada Maharaja prays that Lord Nrsimhadeva will kill any demonlike desires within his heart. Lord Nrsimhadeva can offer us that form of protection.

Devotees tend to pray to Lord Nrsimhadeva to protect their material bodies. When we are in danger, we call out His name. Srila Sridhara Svami prays to Lord Nrsimhadeva as follows in his commentary on the *Srimad-Bhagavatam:* “Let me offer my respectful obeisances to Lord Nrsimhadeva, who is always enlightening His devotee Prahlada Maharaja from within the heart and who always kills the nescience which attacks His devotees... Nrsimhadeva’s mercy is distributed everywhere just like the moonshine, and His face is just like that of a lion. Therefore, let me offer my obeisances unto Lord Nrsimhadeva again and again and again. ”

Lord Nrsimhadeva kills the nescience which attacks His devotees. He also manifests Himself in the pure devotee’s heart. While in the devotee’s heart, He tears out the desire for fruitive activities. “You possess nails and teeth just like thunderbolts. ” In our prayers, we say that Lord Nrsimhadeva has fingernails like chisels. We can pray, “Please, Lord, take those nails and rip out all my desires for fruitive activities. ”

Lord Nrsimhadeva is certainly kindly disposed toward His devotees; therefore, the Lord’s protection extends beyond protection of the material body. Lord Nrsimhadeva will actually protect us from the attack of nescience. We should always be afraid of nescience’s attack. Nescience is deeply rooted in the psyche; material desires extend deep within the heart. These desires are reactions to past sinful activities. *The Nectar of Devotion* describes the three causes of distress. The first is *papam,* sinful activities; the second is *bijam,* material desires; and the third is *avidya,* ignorance. Sinful activities have two types of reactions, *prarabdha* (manifest) and *aprarabdha* (unmanifest). *Prarabdha* reactions are those sinful reactions which we can see now. Our particular birth with all that means is called *prarabdha-karma.* Srila Prabhupada lists some of the negative reactions in *The Nectar of Devotion:* legal implication, low birth, and chronic disease. A chronic disease is difficult to cure. It is better to catch a disease before it gets to that point. *Prarabdha-karma* is already at that point and is therefore difficult to “cure. ”

But devotional service is so powerful that it can even remove the reactions that have already manifest. Srila Rupa Gosvami quotes a third canto verse from the *Srimad-Bhagavatam* to prove his point. This verse was spoken by Devahuti and describes how one who engages in glorifying the Supreme Personality of Godhead by always offering obeisances and always chanting His name is qualified to perform Vedic sacrifices even if he is a dog-eater. To take birth as a dog-eater is a low birth. Devotional service can overcome this karma.

In a sense, the manifest reactions are already on the surface. We also have to clear the reactions that lie below the surface as *aprarabdha-karma.* There are two kinds of unmanifest reactions. The first is the proclivity to engage in a previously performed sinful act, and the other is to meditate on the act until we are driven to perform it. Someone who has been dishonest may feel a proclivity to be dishonest again. Still, he has not acted upon it. This is called *kutam,* sinful proclivity. When his mind becomes attached to a particular act and he begins to plan how he will do it, that is called *bijam.* When we actually engage in the sinful act, then the cycle repeats itself, and we become caught in an endless cycle of sinful activities, sinful reactions, the drive to commit the same sinful activities, followed by more sinful activities, and again, sinful reactions. All these actions and reactions are stockpiled in the heart. This is why we say that sin is deeply rooted in the heart.

Such sins and their reactions cannot be immediately neutralized. It would be nice if they could. But there is no atonement that can uproot the unburnt seeds of sinful desire in the heart. Only a rare soul who has attained unalloyed devotion to the Supreme Lord can uproot these weeds.

The truth is, removing these deep-rooted desires is not something we can do by ourselves. We have to pray for Lord Nrsimhadeva’s protection as Prahlada Maharaja does in this verse. Uprooting material desires is the main protection Lord Nrsimhadeva offers His devotee. In effect, while uprooting the weeds, Lord Nrsimhadeva kills the attack of nescience. This nescience is the “demon” in our case.

Prahlada Maharaja prays, “Please protect me from the demons. ” He was not afraid of his father; he is talking about the demonlike desires within his heart. As Prahlada Maharaja was harassed by Hiranyakasipu until Lord Nrsimhadeva killed him, so we will be harassed by demonlike desires until Lord Nrsimhadeva kills them completely. The *Bhagavad-gita* tells us that a transcendentalist experiences peace. Even though so many rivers may enter an ocean, the ocean remains still, unaffected by the river waters. Similarly, a person engaged in devotional service is not disturbed by the desires that flow on the surface of his mind.

Devotees are always being attacked by demons, and we should not expect that our desires will disappear instantly. If we take shelter in our materialistic desires, we cannot expect to be protected from them. We must pray to the Lord constantly for freedom. Only Krsna can protect us. Krsna assures us that He *will* protect any devotee who surrenders to Him.

*tava kara-kamala-vare nakham adbhuta-srngam*  
*dalita-hiranyakasipu-tanu-bhrngam*  
*kesava dhrta-narahari-rupa jaya jagadisa hare*

This verse comes from the *Nrsimha Purana.* We chant it every day. This verse states that Lord Nrsimhadeva is here, Lord Nrsimhadeva is there; Lord Nrsimhadeva is inside, and Lord Nrsimhadeva is outside. Especially, Lord Nrsimhadeva is within the heart. When Hiranyakasipu asked Prahlada, “Is your God in this pillar? ”Prahlada said, “My God is everywhere, so He is certainly in this pillar. ” Prahlada could see his worshipable Lord everywhere. He could see Him without and he also could see Him within his own heart, because Lord Nrsimhadeva is always enlightening His devotee from within the heart. Krsna says, “For one who sees Me everywhere and sees everything in Me, I am never lost nor is he ever lost to Me. ” If we can learn to see the Lord everywhere, we will never lose Him, nor will Krsna ever lose us. We will always be within the Lord’s vision.

If we are praying to Lord Nrsimhadeva, we are within His vision. We should then understand what kind of protection we require. The most dangerous thing we face is material desire within the heart. It is said that in Kali-yuga, all the demons live within our body. We may be thinking that all the demons live somewhere outside of ourselves, but it is not true. The worst demons live in our own hearts. These demons attack us continuously. We may be afraid of a future attack of the external demons, but how many times a day are we attacked by the internal ones? Only the Lord can protect us from them.

Until the Lord actually kills these internal demons, we will be harassed. Therefore we should offer our respectful obeisances to Lord Nrsimhadeva and beg Him for His mercy. Any devotee aspiring to be free of material desires should offer his respectful prayers to Lord Nrsimhadeva just as Prahlada Maharaja did in this verse. Do not be afraid of the pain that these demons’ removal will cause. We want the Lord to rip these demons out of the heart. They are so deep-rooted that it will not be easy. We are very attached to them. When we use the word “rip, ” we imply that their removal will not be easy—not because the Lord lacks the strength to do it, but because we are so attached that we will try to hold onto our desires. We may even be afraid to offer the prayer. What if the Lord hears us and we lose what we think is dear?

Sometimes a devotee offers this prayer without being aware of its import. He may begin to see the Lord’s response—his attachments being destroyed. He may wonder why all these things are happening to him. He may even renege on his prayer, deciding he’s not ready for such mercy. He plans to restate the prayer when he’s older and can enjoy his senses less. Prahlada Maharaja preached on these exact points to his classmates. He told them to become Krsna conscious while they were young. By the time we are old, these attachments are going to be very difficult to remove.

Srila Prabhupada explains that if we want to make spiritual advancement, we have to realize that the material attachments are holding us back. One who sincerely desires to become free of material desires should pray to Lord Nrsimhadeva as Prahlada Maharaja prayed. The Lord will then protect that devotee from ignorance and fruitive desire. Ignorance is the root cause of sinful life. If we pull up weeds from the garden but do not get the root, then the plant will grow back. If we do not remove the root of ignorance from our heart, then the sinful desires will grow back.

Prahlada Maharaja prays, “Please appear in our hearts and drive away our ignorance so that by Your mercy we may become fearless in the struggle for existence in this material world. ” This is the only way to become fearless; ignorance must be completely driven out by the Lord’s mercy.

Certainly the Lord will protect us if we ask Him for His mercy. Let us remember these pastimes on these auspicious days and understand what they mean for us. *Srnvatam sva-kathah krsnah punya-sravanakirtanah*: Krsna, who is situated within the heart, destroys the material desires in the core of the heart of that person who develops the desire to hear about His transcendental pastimes.

## The Counselor System

## We must care for devotees

**Ista-gosthi with brahmacaris August 8, 1999**

Some time ago, I was asked to speak about the counselor system before an assembly of devotees. This topic is personally important to me. I want to express my conviction about this counselor system, not based upon my position as an authority but from a personal and open hearted stance of someone who has had twenty-seven years of experience in ISKCON. If you see my experience as valid, then maybe when I open my heart, you will be influenced in some small way and come to understand why I feel the counselor system is important. Therefore, I am not speaking in my capacity as Moscow’s GBC, and I am not here to force you to accept this system. I have come to realize that GBC hammering doesn’t open anyone’s heart. Frankly, I'm tired of trying to force people, and I put my hammer away years ago. If I ever use a hammer again, it will only be to hit a nail. I do hope, however, to open a few minds and perhaps some hearts as well. Please forget for the time being that I am your GBC; this is only Niranjana Swami speaking to you.

I have seen in my years in the Krsna consciousness movement that devotees tend to get diverted. Sometimes the diversion is obvious and sometimes it’s not so obvious.

When I think back to why I joined ISKCON, I remember that I joined so I could develop love for Lord Krsna. I wanted to learn how to become Krsna conscious. When I joined ISKCON, the organization was not much developed. Srila Prabhupada had established a number of temples around the world, and had just created the GBC. I could see that there was a structure, but our temples were based upon simple principles.

For example, there were no *bhakta or* members programs. I don’t even think we used the word “department” in those days. Each temple had a temple president, temple commander, and a temple full of *brahmacaris* and *brahmacarinis.* There were householders living nearby too, but most of the devotees were single and lived in the temple. Our lives were simple and austere because the movement had not developed enough for us to have much money.

During my first winter, the temple had no heat. All the *brahmacaris* were issued coats. Since the temple didn’t have much money, the coats were of poor quality. They probably cost about twenty dollars each, which is all we had to spend. At night, we would all zip up our coats, and for those of us who had sleeping bags, we would climb into them and go to sleep. We woke up for *mangala-arati,* chanted our *japa,* ate breakfast (a slice of orange, a few chickpeas, and a bowl of oatmeal), cleaned the *asrama,* then went out on *harinama.* We followed the same routine almost every day. We ate lunch while out on *harinama* (bread and potatoes), sitting down to eat outside or inside the subway station. We returned to the temple around 5: 00 p. m. Sometimes the *harinama* party consisted of two devotees; sometimes everyone was able to go out—all four or five of us. There were very few devotees in those days, at least at our temple. In the evenings, we showered, attended *sundara-arati, Bhagavad-gita* class, and went to sleep. Every day was exactly the same.

Book distribution increased a little the year I joined, but it had not yet become a main service in ISKCON. Activities were centered around deity worship, *harinama, prasadam* distribution, and BTG distribution. Srila Prabhupada had emphasized these activities for our Krsna conscious development, and therefore life was very simple and regulated. In this simple environment, we learned how to transform our previously complicated lives into simple Krsna conscious experiences.

The temple devotees in those days were like members of one family. We had our difficulties, and sometimes we argued. We also had our financial struggles. But we shared the idea that we wanted to help one another advance in Krsna consciousness. In our efforts to help one another, we were able to take advantage of one another’s strengths.

I had many friends at the temple, including the temple president. Sometimes all he did was pat me on the back, but I appreciated his concern and it encouraged me to surmount the difficulties. For example, when we did begin book distribution, I found it difficult, but I always felt the temple president was watching over me. It made me feel safe. Because I felt safe, I was happy despite my struggles. Someone actually cared about me.

I’ll never forget those beginning years in Krsna consciousness. I’m happy that my temple president from those days is still active in ISKCON today (he is now preaching in Italy). I’m also grateful to him for the care he gave me. In fact, I wanted to invite him to Moscow this year. Had I not had such a caring temple president (and *sankirtana* leader), I don’t know if I would have made it through the difficulties.

When the temple president recommended me for initiation, Srila Prabhupada wrote back accepting me and giving me my new name, Niranjana dasa. At the end of the letter he wrote, “Keep him carefully now. ” Prabhupada said that the president should take good care of me because Krsna consciousness means to create ideal men. Unfortunately, I didn’t see that letter at my initiation; I didn’t see it for another fifteen years. I don’t know why the temple president didn’t show it to me, but after reading it and looking back, I was glad that that particular temple president followed Prabhupada’s instruction.

The reason I am telling you this is to point out that the care I received from the devotees in my own temple during those early years in Krsna consciousness was vital to my success in ISKCON. It is that care that helped me remain committed to the devotional life. The care was not forced—forced care doesn’t work—but care that genuinely came from the devotees’ hearts. These devotees actually cared about *me.* Not only that, but what made their care so attractive is that they actually wanted me to be Krsna conscious. It didn’t feel exploitative. That is, he didn’t care about me because he needed someone to wash the pots, and he didn’t care about me because I was the temple handyman. He didn’t care about me because I was the only person with a driver’s license or because I could distribute books. Although someone may doubt what I’m saying, I know what I felt at the time.

How did this devotee manifest his concern? Whenever he came up to me, he would take the time to tell me something about Prabhupada. In those days we had few meetings. I don’t remember ever going to the temple president’s office for a meeting. Our meetings took place whenever he chose to approach me and pat me on the back. He would then sit down and talk for a few minutes. I would feel encouraged, cleansed, and inspired by his association; I would feel ready to go on with my service, because I knew we were all struggling *together.* I was not alone.

In those days, our temple was suffering from financial difficulty. We were only distributing a few BTGs at twenty-five cents each.
