If someone distributed twenty magazines in a day, we thought that was phenomenal and we would speak about it all day long. Still, the temple president did not push us to go out and make money. Instead, he would come up to us and tell us little things about Prabhupada and his mission. We ourselves developed the inspiration to keep the temple going.

Naturally, under such a leader, our temple began to grow— the whole movement began to grow—and we began to acquire larger buildings for our temples. Devotees began to join, book distribution began to increase, which brought in more money, and we were able to expand ourselves into many departments and programs. Srila Prabhupada encouraged us to expand, because he had the vision that Krsna consciousness should take over the world. That would only happen if his disciples were willing to give Krsna consciousness to others. Preaching really became the heart of the movement. Sometimes we say that book distribution is the heart of the movement, but we should understand that book distribution is synonymous with the distribution of Krsna consciousness. That is, the heart of ISKCON is giving Krsna consciousness to others. Srila Prabhupada taught us to be selfless about that. He wanted us to renounce personal ambition for profit and fame and sincerely serve the order of guru and Krsna by preaching.

I have seen over the years that emphasis has sometimes been placed more on external expansion than on internal cultivation, but during his presence, Srila Prabhupada expected us to do both: remain spiritually strong while giving Krsna consciousness widely to others. For Srila Prabhupada, internal and external were the same; he was completely self-realized. He did not see anything that was not connected to Krsna. Therefore, there was no conflict between internal cultivation and the external sharing of spiritual life with others. Those of us who are not fully Krsna conscious, however, need to make some distinction in order to put due emphasis on *sadhana. If* we equate Krsna consciousness only with preaching, we will gradually become attached to the benefits we receive as preachers— fame, adoration, and distinction. Visvanatha Cakravarti calls this *taranga-rangini,* enjoying (*rangini*) the small waves (*taranga*) on the *bhakti* shore.

We see this sometimes among our members. A devotee performs steady service, but then becomes attached to the comforts of his position. A *brahmacari* may say that he has served selflessly for so many years in the *asrama,* living in a room with many devotees. When he has gained some seniority, perhaps he only has to share his room with one or two others. He begins to enjoy the facility and forget the simplicity that austerity provides for his Krsna consciousness. Such enjoyment can lead him into falldown or a simple loss of his position.

Perhaps another devotee has worked hard for many years and gradually risen in the temple to the point where he or she is able to control money. Perhaps this devotee begins to think he or she can enjoy some of the money, or at least the prestige that comes with the control over the money. This devotee has begun to forget Krsna.

Narada Muni has prescribed the details of *brahmacari* life in the *Srimad-Bhagavatam. A brahmacari* is supposed to practice controlling his senses. He must also be submissive and develop an attitude of friendship toward his spiritual master. With a firm vow, the *brahmacari* should live in the *asrama* only for his guru’s benefit. He should also study the *Vedas,* chant his assigned mantras, and daily offer his obeisances to his spiritual master both before and after his study. He should do all these things with great respect. Narada Muni also states that a *brahmacari* should be active, expert, and faithful. He should be well-behaved and simple. He should never collect or eat more than necessary. His life is meant to be simple. *Brahmacari* life is meant for education and discipline.

In the same section, Narada prescribes that the *brahmacari* should daily beg alms on his guru’s behalf. When he returns, he should give everything he has collected to the spiritual master. If the spiritual master does not call him to eat that day, the *brahmacari* will fast. That is his discipline.

Those of us who are disciplined by nature will find it easier to accept the guru’s discipline; for those of us who are not selfdisciplined, the task will be harder. But *asrama* life means living simply and austerely both to carry out the guru’s preaching mission and to practice attentive *sadhana.* Life in the *asrama* is meant to be dedicated to the guru; to live in the *asrama* requires that we become selfless.

These are the reasons why Srila Prabhupada established *asrama.* Still, I have seen over the years—and it has been painful to see—that people are living in the *asrama* without knowing or remembering these principles. We can ask anyone who has been a *brahmacari* and become a householder how much more simple their life was before marriage. What were the anxieties of *brahmacari* life? That he couldn’t find his *kaupins* or his *dhoti.* Sometimes a *brahmacari* may feel he didn’t receive enough *prasadam.* These are simple anxieties compared to the anxiety of maintaining a wife and children and all the accumulations of household life. I see this clearly when I visit my householder godbrothers in the United States. One godbrother told me that his thirteen-year-old daughter was associating with nondevotees and writing letters to boys. He thought he would have to get her married soon. His son has no attraction for Krsna consciousness but simply spends his time playing computer games. Children are a big anxiety.

*Brahmacari* life is meant to be simple. A *brahmacari* should study and should learn how to preach Krsna consciousness. He should not be absorbed in economic development. Economic development is for *grhasthas.* When a *brahmacari* goes out on book distribution, he should go out to distribute Krsna consciousness and beg alms on behalf of his spiritual master. He does not go out to make money. He goes out with service on his mind. We see that *brahmacaris* sometimes forget the purpose of their activities. If we lose our focus, however, we will also lose the simple satisfaction that comes from service.

This becomes even worse when he begins to look around and sees that no one cares about him. He might see that people are only interested in ordering him around, but they don’t care about his struggles or his Krsna conscious development. ISKCON now has so many departments and programs, and all of them have to be maintained. Leaders are looking for people to do the work. How will they maintain it all without help? Devotees feel pushed to work.

I would like to say that devotees don’t mind helping when they feel cared for. Inspiration comes from personal and loving dealings. As an institution, it is easy to become so caught up in the externals that we forget about one another. Then devotees start feeling that they are dispensable. No one cares whether they are there or not. Instead, they care only about how the bills will be paid.

From that, these devotees begin to wonder what happened to the ideals that were established by Srila Prabhupada. Prabhupada did not create the movement just so that we could preach and hold big programs but not help one another advance in spiritual life. If we want to help devotees remain inspired and dedicated, we have to care for them.

A phenomenon that has arisen in these days of institutionalism is devotees moving from temple to temple. In the years when I was a new devotee, we did very little of that. We certainly wouldn’t leave a temple without authorization. If we don’t like the temple where we lived, we wouldn’t simply pack our bags and try out another temple. Of course, we cannot recreate the “old” days in ISKCON, nor do I think they should be brought back. What I would like to see brought back is the spirit of cooperation and respect that we had for one another. *That* can be recreated—that very spirit Srila Prabhupada gave us and wanted to see in his temples. We can be simple, we can preach, and we can help one another remember the goal of life.

Srila Prabhupada says clearly that the temples are not meant for lazy men who are interested only in eating and sleeping.

I realize that temple presidents are not as easily accessible as they were when I was a young devotee. Still, we can be intelligent and apply the principle as is possible in today’s ISKCON. The devotees who have taken responsibility as counselors have been selected by the mass of devotees. They were selected not because they were administrators, but because they can be trusted and are willing to show others that they care. These devotees can teach what they have learned about Krsna consciousness, and are willing to help keep things in focus. In this way, you and they can help build a common sense of struggling together both to push on the preaching and to advance in Krsna consciousness. Common struggle creates a bond. No one should struggle alone; everyone should try to help one another.

One of the principles of spiritual life is to reveal one’s mind in confidence and to inquire confidentially. I can only appeal to you to pursue this system and make it work. I am not going to force you. I am simply saying that based on my twenty-seven years of experience in Krsna consciousness, I am convinced that establishing counselors is the best way to create a mood similar to the one I described at the beginning of this discussion.

When we apply this system, some devotees may realize that they can no longer live in the temple. I am prepared to accept that fewer devotees may live in the temple because at least the temple will be a place where everyone who is living there *wants* to be there and wants to struggle together. The atmosphere in the temple will become more inspired and the devotees’ enthusiasm will become contagious. It will spread to those living outside because the temple devotees’ lives will be based on the very simple and important principle of giving Krsna consciousness to others while simultaneously giving it to themselves.

In the early days, we had no departments. Now we do. How will we find a united ground upon which to struggle together? We should first understand that whether we are doing book distribution, life membership, *prasadam* distribution, or the cafe (we have so many departments), that each of these departments is dedicated to bringing resources—whether people, money, or something else—into the temple. We should also understand that the whole point in maintaining a temple is to provide a place in which devotees can become Krsna conscious and a base from which they can give Krsna consciousness to others. This is the basis upon which Srila Prabhupada established the spiritual programs: *arati, harinama, Bhagavatam* and *Bhagavad-gita* classes, etc. Attending these programs is part of our simplicity. It used to be that everyone would take a turn out on *harinama.* The temple devotees and the *sankirtana* devotees would take turns doing the deity worship so everyone got a chance to preach. On weekends, the entire temple would go out. This helped to unify the temple, because everyone remained focused as to what the temple was for.

We have to struggle if we want to become Krsna conscious. We should not equate Krsna consciousness simply with external duties. Sometimes devotees think that if they go on doing their service, one day they will naturally become Krsna conscious. But even while they are serving, their minds are rejecting the system. They don’t like their service and they want to do something else. Or perhaps they don’t like the persons with whom they have to serve. How will we wake up from such a state and be Krsna conscious? Do we think that by disliking our service and the other devotees that we will one day wake up to find Krsna embracing us? Prabhupada never taught such a thing.

Everyone should experience how much of a struggle it is to inspire Krsna consciousness in others during class. The *Bhagavatam* speakers are supposed to think, “Somehow I have to inject Krsna consciousness into their hearts! ” It is not so simple; it is a struggle. The speaker has to pray, “Krsna, please empower me. Let me speak in a way that will cause others to remember You. ” That struggle should be present in everyone’s mind. All the devotees in the temple should be learning to lecture in this mood. The speaker has to struggle to instruct, and the audience has to struggle to hear. Isn’t that what took place in the forest of Naimisaranya? The audience asked questions for the benefit of the entire world. They were thinking of themselves, but of others too. This is the sacrifice we must perform together. If we do it, it will bind us together into a unified movement.

We cannot spread Krsna consciousness if we are not feeling Krsna conscious. If we do not feel Krsna conscious, we will discuss only the externals. Our activities, even though grounded in Krsna consciousness, will not feel satisfying. We’ll think we are making money instead of distributing transcendental knowledge. Our efforts will not have any significant spiritual impact on others. We must take care of ourselves and each other. We should not exclude ourselves from the process of becoming Krsna conscious, even in the name of preaching. *Sankirtana* is meant to be performed together.

Therefore, I can only appeal to you to take part in this system, to attend regular meetings with the counselors, and to openly discuss your doubts or questions. You should also inquire about your personal concerns. The counselor system has not been designed as a place to address concerns about others. In fact, I recently gave a seminar on *prajalpa.* When I finished the seminar, it became clear in my mind that Srila Bhaktivinoda Thakura gave straightforward instructions about when we should talk about others and when we should not. Srila Bhaktivinoda Thakura explains that for one who is practicing *bhakti* but whose mind is absorbed in speaking about others out of pride or envy, he will never be able to fix his mind on Krsna’s lotus feet. These meetings are meant so that we can gain benefit for ourselves, not complain about others.

I feel that I have spoken to you not only for your benefit but for my own. I tried to open my heart to you so that I could explain some of the things that are causing me pain. It is painful to see Srila Prabhupada’s very simple principles being lost. Srila Prabhupada gave us the perfect formula for becoming Krsna conscious. It works. It *will* work if we practice it sincerely. Srila Prabhupada often emphasized the simplicity of Krsna consciousness. Please don’t try to complicate it; don’t try to make it more than it actually is.

Srila Prabhupada once said in a lecture, “Don’t be overintelligent. Don’t think that you know more than your spiritual master. ” We should simply hear and repeat our guru’s words, and if we become over-intelligent, then the whole movement will be lost. To be simple includes not complicating Srila Prabhupada’s instructions to us. If we follow the simple process, we will feel ourselves becoming Krsna conscious. We should center our lives on this process.
